18

19

Yes.  I submitted pre-filed testimony in the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases.     
10 
11 
Q.
What is the purpose of the Panel’s testimony? 
12 
A.
The Panel’s testimony addresses programs and performance metrics related to the 
13 
safe operation of the Companies’ gas systems during the 12-month period ending 
14 
March 31, 2021 (“Rate Year”), and the three subsequent 12-month periods ending 
15 
March 31, 2022 (“Data Year 1”), March 31, 2023 (“Data Year 2”), and March 31, 
16 
2024 (“Data Year 3”) (Data Year 1, Data Year 2, and Data Year 3 are collectively 
17 
referred to as the “Data Years”).  The Panel also presents a forecast for the 15-
18 
month period (“Bridge Period”) between December 31, 2018, which is the end of 
19 
the Companies’ Historic Test Year, and the beginning of the Rate Year.   
20 
21 
3708


--- Page 19 ---
6 of 64 
Specifically, the Panel’s testimony addresses the Companies’ proposals for (i) 
1 
enhanced and incremental gas safety and compliance programs, including programs 
2 
providing additional training, safety inspections, and contractor oversight; (ii) gas 
3 
safety performance metrics; and (iii) an accelerated procedure for addressing 
4 
inactive accounts.   
5 
6 
Q.
Before discussing the specific programs presented in this rate filing, please 
7 
discuss the Companies’ commitment to safety and compliance.  
8 
A.
The Companies strive for continuous improvement in safety performance by 
9 
identifying, assessing, and managing risks on the gas system – and making prudent 
10 
and necessary investments in gas infrastructure to address these risks.  As discussed 
11 
by KEDNY and KEDLI’s respective Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panels 
12 
(“GIOP”), KEDNY will invest more than $3.8 billion in gas infrastructure in the 
13 
Rate Year and Data Years to enhance gas pipeline safety, meet increasing 
14 
regulatory requirements, and maintain and improve the reliability of its gas 
15 
distribution system, including the retirement of more than 300 miles of leak prone 
16 
pipe (“LPP”) during that period.  Similarly, KEDLI is investing more than $2.3 
17 
billion in its gas network to support safe operations and enhance system 
18 
performance, including the retirement of more than 600 miles of LPP during the 
19 
Rate Year and Data Years.  The Companies are also investing in pipeline and 
20 
transmission station integrity management programs, automatic shut off valves, and 
21 
3709


--- Page 20 ---
7 of 64 
other programs to enhance public safety.  To ensure sufficient oversight and quality 
1 
control over this expanding portfolio of capital investments, the Companies are 
2 
proposing to implement an enhanced inspection program that will ultimately deploy 
3 
dedicated field inspectors to oversee and inspect the work of every construction 
4 
crew. 
5 
6 
Importantly, investments in the gas system must be supported by technology and 
7 
processes that enable the Companies to manage their assets and workforce and meet 
8 
heightened safety and regulatory expectations.  Accordingly, as discussed in the 
9 
testimony of Companies’ Witness Christopher J. Connolly, National Grid has 
10 
undertaken a comprehensive effort to evaluate the technology and business 
11 
processes that support its gas businesses (referred to as the Gas Business 
12 
Enablement (“GBE”) Program).  Among the core investments of the GBE Program 
13 
are new asset and work management, scheduling, geographic information system, 
14 
and field mobility solutions designed to improve the Companies’ gas safety and 
15 
operational performance.   
16 
17 
Q.
Please describe the Companies’ safety and compliance performance.   
18 
Despite the need for technology investments and increasing work plan 
19 
requirements, KEDNY and KEDLI have historically performed well and met all 
20 
safety performance targets.  Each year, the Department of Public Service Staff 
21 
3710


--- Page 21 ---
8 of 64 
(“Staff”) issues an annual report on local distribution companies’ (“LDCs”) gas 
1 
reliability performance in relation to established metrics.  Highlights of KEDNY 
2 
and KEDLI’s performance, as reported by Staff’s reports through 2017 include: (i) 
3 
an overall reduction in the damages due to excavator error since 2015; (ii) a 
4 
reduction in emergency response times to gas leak and odor calls within 30 minutes 
5 
every year since 2015; and (iii) a reduction in the backlog of total leaks every year 
6 
since 2013.  The Staff report for 2018 will not be released until later in 2019; 
7 
however, the Companies recently reported their performance against safety metrics 
8 
to the Commission for calendar year (“CY”) 2018, as part of the annual gas safety 
9 
performance metric filing, and have again outperformed every safety metric target 
10 
(e.g., LPP Removal, Leak Management, Damage Prevention, Emergency 
11 
Response).  
12 
13 
Each year, Staff audits the Companies’ compliance with gas safety regulations in 
14 
the field, as well as the Companies’ records and documentation.  Since 2016, 
15 
KEDNY and KEDLI have shown year-over-year improvements with respect to 
16 
performance against gas safety compliance requirements, as shown in Table 1.       
17 
3711


--- Page 22 ---
9 of 64 
Table 1:  KEDNY & KEDLI Compliance Trends (Field and Records Audits)  
1 
2 
3 
4 
While KEDNY and KEDLI have demonstrated significant progress in this area, the 
5 
Companies acknowledge that further improvement is needed and, therefore, are 
6 
advancing various compliance programs in these rate filings, including a 
7 
3712


--- Page 23 ---
10 of 64 
comprehensive pipeline safety management system, enhanced training and 
1 
inspection programs, and enhanced compliance monitoring and documentation 
2 
capabilities through the GBE program. 
3 
4 
Q.
What safety and compliance improvements have the Companies made over the 
5 
last several years? 
6 
Following an independent assessment (“IA”) of National Grid’s safety and 
7 
compliance processes by an external pipeline safety consultant several years ago, 
8 
the Companies have worked toward systematically improving their safety and 
9 
compliance performance.  Over the past several years, the Companies have made 
10 
significant progress in implementing the recommendations of the IA, including 
11

13

3713

compliance by providing clear, end-to-end accountability for gas safety

A. Plastic Fusion Programs
19 
Q.
Please describe the Plastic Fusion Programs. 
20 
3717


--- Page 28 ---
15 of 64 
A.
There are two plastic fusion safety programs in the Companies’ capital investment 
1 
plans, the Plastic Fusions QA/QC Program and a new Plastic Fusion In-Process 
2 
Inspections Program.  Both programs are in response to increased focus on the risk 
3 
of plastic fusion failures following the 2014 East Harlem, New York incident and 
4 
the Commission’s new, expansive requirements regarding plastic fusion 
5 
compliance.   
6 
7 
The QA/QC Program is mandated by the Commission’s “Order Adopting Further 
8 
Improvements in Plastic Fusion Practices on Natural Gas Systems” issued on May 
9 
18, 2018 in Case 14-G-0212 (“Plastic Fusion Order”).  Under this program, the 
10 
Companies conduct re-dig inspections of a statistically significant number of plastic 
11 
fusions on completed construction jobs within 30 days of installation.  The Plastic 
12 
Fusion QA/QC Program requires capital investments of approximately $3.3 million 
13 
in the Rate Year, $3.4 million in Data Year 1, $3.5 million Data Year 2, and $3.5 
14 
million in Data Year 3 for KEDNY.  For KEDLI, the program requires capital 
15 
investments of $1 million in the Rate Year and each of the Data Years.  The costs 
16 
for the Plastic Fusion Program are shown separately in Exhibit __ (GIOP-1) for 
17 
transparency, but will ultimately be accounted for in the total unit costs of the 
18 
various main and service replacement programs.      
19 
20 
3718


--- Page 29 ---
16 of 64 
The Plastic Fusion In-Process Inspection Program is a supplemental program to 
1 
enhance the safety and reliability of plastic fusion work.  Under this program, 
2 
construction crews performing plastic fusions will send photographs of the fusion to 
3 
a contractor, ControlPoint, to perform a remote, real-time inspection.  The 
4 
inspection includes evaluation of stab depth, surface clearing, scraping, alignment, 
5 
fusion time, and temperature compensation.  The findings from the inspection are 
6 
communicated to the construction crews prior to completion of the project, allowing 
7 
for any corrections to be made in real-time and before a project is backfilled.  A 
8 
National Grid affiliate company in the United Kingdom recently adopted an in-
9 
process inspection program using the same contractor, and the program has proved 
10 
effective in detecting potential issues and enabling corrective actions prior to work 
11 
being completed.  In-process inspections are not required by the Plastic Fusion 
12 
Order but have been shown to be a cost-effective means to reduce risk related to 
13 
plastic fusions.  Because of the significant benefits of this program, the Companies 
14 
are beginning to conduct in-process inspections during fiscal year (“FY”) 2020. 
15 
The capital investment plan includes approximately $0.3 million for KEDNY in 
16 
each of the Rate Year and Data Years and $0.6 million for KEDLI in each of the 
17 
Rate Year and Data Years (adjusted for inflation).  In addition, there are 
18 
incremental non-labor O&M costs of $0.102 million for KEDNY and $0.203 
19 
million for KEDLI in the Rate Year, inflated through Data Year 3.  The costs for 
20 
the In-process Inspection Program are shown separately in Exhibit __ (GIOP-1) for 
21 
3719


--- Page 30 ---
17 of 64 
transparency, but will ultimately be accounted for in the total unit costs of the 
1 
various main and service replacement programs.

B. Inside Service Line Inspections Program
13 
Q.
Please describe the Inside Service Line Inspection Program and what is 
14 
required to implement the new mandated inside service line inspection 
15 
intervals. 
16 
A.
In 2015, the Commission issued an Order that adopted a new definition of “service 
17 
line” in New York (Case 14-G-0357).  Under the former definition in New York’s 
18 
pipeline safety regulations (16 NYCRR section 255.3(29)), the service line 
19 
extended “to the first accessible fitting” inside the customer’s building where a 
20 
meter is located.  Under the new definition, the service line now extends “to the 
21 
3720


--- Page 31 ---
18 of 64 
outlet of the customer meter or at the connection to a customer’s piping, whichever 
1 
is further downstream where a meter is located within the building.”  While the 
2 
Commission adopted the new service line definition in 2015, it stayed 
3 
implementation pending development of a plan to address the new leak survey and 
4 
corrosion inspection requirements for inside facilities.  In April 2017, the 
5 
Commission issued its “Order Establishing Statewide Inspection Schedules and 
6 
Procedural Requirements” in Case 15-G-0244 (“Inside Service Inspection Order”) 
7 
that established mandated leak survey and corrosion inspection intervals for inside 
8 
services.  Citing the 2015 explosion on 2nd Avenue in Manhattan, which involved 
9 
unauthorized tampering with utility gas facilities on the inside of a building, the 
10 
Inside Service Line Inspection Order set an aggressive schedule for utilities to 
11 
complete baseline leak surveys and inspections, and shorter continuous inspection 
12 
intervals thereafter.  For example, for inside services located in business districts, 
13 
leak surveys were to be completed by the end of CY 2018 and re-inspections are 
14 
required to be completed once every year thereafter.  For non-business districts, 
15 
baseline leak surveys are to be completed by April of 2020, and re-inspections must 
16 
occur every three years thereafter.  Baseline corrosion inspections, regardless of 
17 
location, also must be completed by April of 2020, and must be repeated on three-
18 
year intervals.  The Companies began to ramp-up implementation of the Inside 
19 
Service Line Inspection Program during CY 2017 and CY 2018.  Additional work 
20 
is required to timely complete baseline and ongoing inspections.     
21 
3721


--- Page 32 ---
19 of 64 
Q.
What are the projected incremental costs to implement the Inside Service Line 
1 
Inspection Program for KEDNY and KEDLI in the Rate Year and Data 
2 
Years? 
3 
A.
More frequent leak surveys and corrosion inspections are required in the Rate Year 
4 
and Data Years compared to the Historic Test Year to meet the compliance 
5 
requirements in the Inside Service Line Inspection Order.  This requires incremental 
6 
O&M expense for additional inspections and documentation requirements, 
7 
incremental FTEs (field inspectors) to manage inspection and quality control of 
8 
contractor work, and a modest reactive capital program to address service 
9 
replacements required when the surveys and inspections reveal substandard 
10 
conditions.  The impact of these changes is greater in KEDNY’s service territory 
11 
because it is more urban with more apartment buildings and other multi-story 
12 
buildings with meters located inside.  Based on the schedule of work to be 
13 
completed each year, KEDNY requires non-labor O&M expense of approximately 
14 
$5.900 million in the Rate Year, inflated through Data Year 3.  In addition, 3.5 
15 
incremental field inspector FTEs are needed in each of the Rate Year and Data 
16 
Years.  KEDLI requires incremental non-labor O&M of approximately $0.135 
17 
million in the Rate Year, inflated through Data Year 3.  In addition, 1.5 incremental 
18 
FTE field inspectors are needed in each of the Rate Year and Data Years.  
19 
20 
3722


--- Page 33 ---
20 of 64

1 
issues identified during inside service inspections, extrapolated to the level of

D. Enhanced Inactive Accounts Program
4 
Q.
Please describe the Companies’ improvements to the inactive accounts process 
5 
over the past several years.   
6 
A.
The Companies have implemented inactive account process improvements that 
7 
considerably mitigate the risks of inactive accounts (i.e., accounts without an active 
8 
customer of record).  Over the last several years, the Companies have implemented 
9 
various process improvements for addressing inactive accounts, including a revised 
10 
end-to-end process that provides a more structured approach to accessing unlocked 
11 
meters.  The Companies have also enhanced their call center procedures, scripts, 
12 
and training; dedicated additional resources to support the inactive account process; 
13 
made investments in the Companies’ web site and customer and work management 
14 
systems to support the inactive account process; established a legal replevin process 
15 
to secure access to gas facilities through court orders; and improved customer 
16 
communications regarding inactive accounts (e.g., occupant/owner letters, post 
17 
cards, door stickers).  Significantly, the Companies perform a site analysis on every 
18 
visit to determine whether conditions, such as trespassing at a vacant premises or 
19 
other observable unsafe condition, warrant the Companies immediately securing the 
20 
service line.  In such instances, the service is cut within 24 hours.   
21 
3725


--- Page 36 ---
23 of 64 
1 
As a result of these process improvements, over the past three years, KEDNY has 
2 
resolved approximately 47 percent of inactive accounts within the first 15 days of 
3 
inactivity, and KEDLI has resolved approximately 89 percent of accounts within 
4 
the first 15 days.  Excluding controlled accounts that are referred to the legal 
5 
replevin process to gain access, between day 45 and day 60, approximately five 
6 
percent of KEDNY’s accounts and 1.6 percent of KEDLI’s accounts remain 
7 
inactive.   
8 
9 
Q.
Are the Companies proposing to modify their inactive accounts processes? 
10 
A.
Yes.  The Companies’ current process provides that all inactive accounts will be 
11 
addressed (e.g., locked or cut in the street) no later than 60 days after the account 
12 
becomes inactive.  In response to Staff’s recommendations for further process 
13 
improvements, the Companies propose to implement a 30-day inactive account 
14 
process.  To mitigate the significant costs of this process change, the Companies 
15 
intend to take a phased approach to shorten the end-to-end inactive account process 
16 
to 45 days by the end of the Rate Year, and to 30 days by the end of Data Year 2.   
17 
18 
Implementation of this process change requires additional resources in field 
19 
operations, CMS, Call Center, Legal, and back office, including additional FTEs. 
20 
In KEDNY’s service territory, significantly more resources are required due to 
21 
3726


--- Page 37 ---
24 of 64 
population density and a higher percentage of transitory customers in rental 
1 
properties.  For KEDNY, the program requires incremental O&M expenses of 
2 
approximately $17.083 million in each of Data Years 1 through 3, plus a total of 
3 
133 FTEs that will be hired between FY 2020 and Data Year 1.  For KEDLI, the 
4

E. I&R Programs
9 
Q.
Please describe I&R and the need for the Companies’ I&R safety proposals. 
10 
A.
I&R involves the safe and reliable operation of critical gas infrastructure used to 
11 
maintain appropriate gas system pressures, including pressure regulating stations, 
12 
heaters, gas quality and odorization equipment, pressure control and pressure 
13 
monitoring, and system automation equipment.  The recent incident in Merrimack 
14 
Valley, Massachusetts involved an over pressure event on the utility’s gas 
15 
distribution system.  Following that incident, at the direction of Staff, New York 
16 
gas utilities immediately reviewed their pressure regulation and overpressure 
17 
protection assets and procedures to identify system risks, identify mitigation 
18 
actions, and consider best practices, including those recommended by Staff.  The 
19 
Companies’ review identified areas where best practices were already in place, and 
20 
recommended improvements and enhancements to improve the safety and 
21 
3727


--- Page 38 ---
25 of 64 
reliability of I&R assets.  As a result, the Companies are proposing a suite of I&R 
1 
safety programs designed to improve procedures, information availability and 
2 
standardization, knowledge transfer, employee competencies, public safety, and 
3 
over pressure risk reduction.  Specifically, the Companies propose two capital 
4 
investment programs – the Distribution Station Over Pressure Protection Program 
5 
and the I&R Training and Test Lab – and three O&M programs – the I&R Control 
6 
Line Survey and GPS Mapping Program, the I&R Site Specific Procedures 
7 
Program, and the I&R Field Trainers Initiative.   
8 
9 
Q.
Please describe the Distribution Station Over Pressure Protection Program. 
10 
A.
This program will directly address over pressurization risk by installing override 
11 
pilots on all low-pressure stations.  If there is an over pressure condition, the 
12 
override pilot operates automatically to limit the amount of outlet pressure from the 
13 
station and prevent the pipeline from over pressurizing until the Companies are able 
14 
to respond to the situation.  The Companies are beginning to install override pilots 
15 
in FY 2020, and the program will continue through the Rate Year and Data Years to 
16 
address all stations.  The capital investment required for this program is 
17 
approximately $0.9 million in the Rate Year and $0.3 million in each of the Data 
18 
Years for KEDNY, and approximately $0.5 million in the Rate Year and $0.3 
19 
million in each of Data Years 1, 2, and 3 for KEDLI.  
20 
21 
3728


--- Page 39 ---
26 of 64 
Q.
Please describe the proposed capital investment in an I&R Training and Test 
1 
Lab. 
2 
A.
I&R employees and supervisors are responsible for operating and maintaining 
3 
complex gas pressure reducing facilities.  In the absence of a dedicated training and 
4 
test lab, I&R employees learn the finer details of equipment maintenance and 
5 
operation on fully functioning in-service stations during on-the-job training under 
6 
the supervision of senior experienced employees.   
7 
8 
The I&R Training and Test Labs proposed by the Companies are functioning 
9 
regulator stations that are not connected to the Companies’ live gas distribution 
10 
systems and operate on compressed air instead of gas.  The training and test labs 
11 
can be set up to simulate any type of operating condition, including failure modes, 
12 
and can provide an opportunity for repeat training on simulated seasonal and 
13 
weather conditions.  In this way, employees can learn to recognize and react to 
14 
abnormal operating conditions in a sandbox environment without risking damage to 
15 
the Companies’ facilities.  The proposed training and test labs also will allow 
16 
employees to learn in a controlled environment while crews working on live 
17 
facilities can fully focus on their work rather than on supervising trainees. 
18 
Moreover, these stations facilitate employee competency assessments in simulated 
19 
operating conditions and provide for consistent training.   
20 
21 
3729


--- Page 40 ---
27 of 64 
The Companies’ assessment of downstate training needs determined that a facility 
1 
in each territory is required due to the number of downstate employees and 
2 
variation of station conditions and training needs between the two Companies. 
3 
There is currently no I&R training and test lab in KEDNY’s service territory. 
4 
KEDLI currently has an I&R training and test lab, but the lab is not sized 
5 
appropriately for its training needs, and the components that are there need to be 
6 
upgraded to ensure the facilities continue to be fit for purpose.  Therefore, the 
7 
Companies propose capital investments to (i) construct a new I&R training and test 
8 
lab in KEDNY’s service territory and (ii) expand KEDLI’s I&R training and test 
9 
lab and refurbish existing equipment.  KEDNY’s program requires a capital 
10 
investment of $0.8 million in the Rate Year and $1 million in Data Year 1. 
11 
KEDLI’s program requires a capital investment of $0.8 million in the Rate Year 
12 
and $0.4 million in Data Year 1.  The I&R Training and Test Lab will be managed 
13 
by the Companies’ I&R department as a direct capital investment program. 
14 
15 
The Companies each require a small yearly O&M expense program, approximately 
16 
$0.075 million in the Rate Year and Data Year 1, increasing to $0.085 million in 
17 
Data Year 2 and Data Year 3.  The program cost is to address wear and tear of parts 
18 
and 
maintenance 
of 
the 
associated 
air 
compressor 
equipment. 
19 
20 
21 
3730


--- Page 41 ---
28 of 64 
Q.
Please describe the I&R Control Line Survey and GPS Mapping Program. 
1 
A.
Control lines are specific piping systems at pressure regulation systems that allow 
2 
the pressure regulating equipment to sense the amount of gas pressure in the 
3 
downstream distribution system.  Damage to control lines can cause connected 
4 
pressure regulators to malfunction that could, under some circumstances, result in 
5 
an over pressure condition on the downstream distribution system.  The Companies’ 
6 
risk assessment determined that legacy documentation of control line facilities 
7 
could be enhanced using updated technologies to mitigate the risk of third-party 
8 
damages.  Indeed, a similar survey and mapping program already underway in 
9 
Niagara Mohawk’s service territory revealed control line facilities that have shifted 
10 
over time due to ground conditions, such that documents no longer reflect correct 
11 
locations, confirming the need for this program.  Thus, the Companies propose a 
12 
program to survey and map the I&R control lines, using GPS technology and 
13 
licensed land surveyors, to obtain exact locations and GPS coordinates.  The costs 
14 
of surveying, excavating (where necessary to locate facilities), and mapping 
15 
regulator station schematics are based on Niagara Mohawk’s surveying project. 
16 
The program requires incremental non-labor O&M in the amount of approximately 
17 
$1.496 million in the Rate Year for KEDNY and $0.500 million in the Rate Year 
18 
for KEDLI.  The program will be completed by the end of the Rate Year; thus, there 
19 
is no spending required in the Data Years.  As discussed above, while the costs for 
20 
this program are included in the revenue requirements, the Companies would be 
21 
3731


--- Page 42 ---
29 of 64 
willing to use the NRAs incurred for gas compliance violations to fund these 
1 
programs.

3 
Q.
Please describe the I&R Site Specific Procedures Program. 
4 
A.
I&R operates about 550 pressure reducing stations requiring periodic inspection, 
5 
maintenance, and repair in KEDNY and KEDLI’s service territories.  The 
6 
procedures currently utilized by I&R technicians require updating.  Specifically, 
7 
many of the procedures lack step-by-step instructions and station-specific detail 
8 
about individual pieces of equipment and individual station configurations.  The 
9 
Site Specific Procedure Program will develop new and update existing procedures 
10 
that are specific to tasks, equipment, and managing emergencies at each individual 
11 
gas regulator station.  Updated site specific procedures will enhance operations 
12 
safety, support mutual aid, create troubleshooting guides, improve training, and 
13 
provide for the retention of institutional technical knowledge when employee turn-
14 
over occurs.   
15 
16 
This initiative will require two incremental I&R engineers (one FTE for each 
17 
company) and one analyst (one-half of an FTE for each company) in each of the 
18 
Rate Year and Data Years to build, document, maintain, and update the procedure 
19 
library.   
20 
21 
3732


--- Page 43 ---
30 of 64 
Q.
Please describe the I&R Field Trainer Initiative. 
1 
A.
The I&R Field Trainer initiative requires two FTEs (one each for KEDNY and 
2 
KEDLI) that will work in the field with I&R Mechanics and Technicians providing 
3

4

and will provide on the spot training and guidance.  Field Trainers will also be 
6 
involved in using the I&R training and test labs and will also serve as subject matter 
7 
experts during classroom training sessions with National Grid Academy instructors.

F. Enhanced Pipeline Safety Management and Damage Prevention Programs
10 
Q.
How do the Companies plan to improve overall gas pipeline safety 
11 
management and damage prevention? 
12 
A.
A cornerstone of the Companies’ long-term plan to effectuate gas pipeline safety 
13 
improvements is the Companies’ commitment to implement the API RP 1173, 
14 
which is a holistic approach to pipeline safety that applies key process safety 
15 
principles to existing risk control programs.  Currently, the Companies’ QA/QC 
16 
Programs conduct field inspections and assessments of traditional mains and service 
17 
work performed by in-house and contractor crews.  Inspections can occur on-site 
18 
during construction activities or occur through a “re-dig” program whereby newly 
19 
constructed underground facilities are exposed and inspected to evaluate 
20 
workmanship and compliance.  This program is distinct from the Contractor Safety 
21 
3733


--- Page 44 ---
31 of 64 
Inspections because it addresses in-house crews as well as contractors.  QA/QC is 
1 
an integral component in assessing compliance with safety related procedures and 
2 
work methods, pipeline safety, and regulatory requirements.  Adoption of RP 1173 
3 
involves conducting more QA/QC inspections, such as more on-site inspections of 
4 
plastic fusions, and expanding the scope of the Companies’ existing QA/QC 
5 
programs for gas construction activities across additional programs executed by 
6 
field operations and customer meter services, including leak management, 
7 
corrosion, and damage prevention.   
8 
9 
The Companies propose to add 15.5 Pipeline Safety Management FTEs (10.4 
10 
dedicated to KEDNY and 5.1 dedicated to KEDLI) to enable the Companies to 
11 
execute this important expansion of QA/QC.   
12 
13 
Q.
Are the Companies making additional improvements to its Damage Prevention 
14 
Program? 
15 
A.
Yes.  To complement RP 1173 implementation, the Companies are also expanding 
16 
their existing Damage Prevention Program.  Damage to gas facilities from third 
17 
parties digging near pipelines is a major cause of gas accidents and a contributing 
18 
factor to some of the worst incidents in the history of the natural gas industry.  In 
19 
addition to the detrimental impacts on safety and reliability, utilities spend millions 
20 
of dollars every year repairing damage caused by these incidents.  Over the last 
21 
3734


--- Page 45 ---
32 of 64 
several years, the Companies have implemented improvements to their Damage 
1 
Prevention programs, including: 
2 
•
Implementing a ticket risk assessment (“TRA”) system that utilizes an
3 
algorithm to evaluate and prioritize one call tickets based on the probability
4 
of damage to the Companies’ underground facilities and enables the
5 
Companies to respond to the tickets with the highest risk of a damage
6 
occurring;
7 
•
creating a Damage Prevention Advisor Program whereby the Companies’
8 
Damage Prevention Advisors meet with and educate municipal personnel,
9 
excavators, and contractors on damage prevention awareness and other
10 
pipeline safety issues; and
11 
•
expanded use of Damage Prevention Advisors to perform regular damage
12 
prevention patrols of the Companies’ entire transmission system pursuant to
13 
code requirements and proactively implementing weekly patrols of
14 
transmission lines in proximity to populated areas and daily weekday patrols
15 
of the most critical transmission lines.
16 
17 
In line with the Companies’ wholistic approach to gas pipeline safety improvement, 
18 
the Companies intend to continue to focus on damage prevention.  The volume of 
19 
one-call tickets has increased annually and is expected to continue increasing in the 
20 
Rate Year and Data Year due to the success of the NY 811 program outreach and 
21 
3735


--- Page 46 ---
33 of 64 
education and increasing construction and development.  As one-call tickets 
1 
increase, the Companies need to increase the number of Damage Prevention 
2 
Advisors to enable the Companies to visit at least 15 percent of one call tickets 
3 
daily.  At current staffing levels, KEDNY’s Damage Prevention Advisors can visit 
4 
only the top five percent of riskiest tickets consistently, and KEDLI’s Damage 
5 
Prevention Advisors can visit only the top ten percent of the riskiest tickets 
6 
consistently.  The Companies propose the addition of two Damage Prevention 
7 
supervisors (1 FTE dedicated to KEDNY and 1 FTE dedicated to KEDLI) in each 
8 
of the Rate Year and Data Years.  Additional contractor resources for expanding the 
9 
scope of the Damage Prevention Advisor Program are needed and require

incremental non-labor O&M expense in the amount of $1.404 million in the Rate 
11 
Year, $1.542 million in Data Year 1, $1.798 million in Data Year 2, and $1.948 
12 
million in Data Year 3 for KEDNY; and $0.779 million in the Rate Year, $0.894 
13 
million in Data Year 1, $1.115 million in Data Year 2, and $1.241 million in Data 
14 
Year 3 for KEDLI.   
15 
16 
Additionally, the expected increase in one-call tickets requires additional contractor 
17 
resources to perform mark outs and operate the TRA system.  To manage the 
18 
expected increased volume of work, KEDNY requires incremental non-labor O&M 
19 
expense in the amount of approximately $0.980 million in the Rate Year, $1.233 
20 
million in Data Year 1, $1.561 million in Data Year 2, and $1.986 million in Data 
21 
3736


--- Page 47 ---
34 of 64 
Year 3; and KEDLI requires approximately $0.225 million in the Rate Year, $0.325 
1 
million in Data Year 1, $0.443 million in Data Year 2, and $0.584 million in Data 
2 
Year 3.  
3 
4 
Q.
Does the expected increase in damage prevention mark out tickets have any 
5 
impact on field operations?  
6 
A.
Yes.  Mark outs that contractor crews are unable to complete due to field 
7 
conditions, such as lack of a tracer wire or discrepancies between field conditions 
8 
and the Companies’ records, are referred to field operations for completion.  These 
9 
mark outs are known as “turn backs.”  As requests for mark outs increase, so do the 
10 
number of mark out turn backs referred to field operations.  Because mark outs are 
11 
anticipated to increase in KEDLI’s service territory during the Rate Year and Data 
12 
Years, KEDLI forecasts incremental non-labor O&M expense in the amount of 
13 
approximately $0.442 million in the Rate Year, $0.734 million in Data Year 1, 
14 
$1.083 million in Data Year 2, and $1.501 million in Data Year 3 for field 
15 
operations to address additional turn backs.

H. Buried Vent Lines Program
17 
Q.
Please explain the Buried Vent Line Program. 
18 
A.
The Companies each estimate that approximately five percent of their respective 
19 
high-pressure services with inside meter sets have regulator vent lines that are 
20 
buried underground.  Buried vent lines are more susceptible to corrosion and 
21 
3738


--- Page 49 ---
36 of 64 
damage because the vent pipes are not coated and do not have cathodic protection. 
1 
Corroded and/or damaged vent lines pose an increased risk for gas migration into a 
2 
building or regulator over-pressurization caused by water intrusion and/or freeze-
3 
ups caused by extreme weather.  In February 2016, KEDNY experienced an 
4 
incident on its system that involved a buried vent line that was found to be corroded 
5 
and could have contributed to an over pressure condition due to freezing water in 
6 
the regulator.  To mitigate the risk of buried vent lines, the Companies propose a 
7 
capital program to systematically address buried vent lines through either re-routing 
8 
of the vent line to above-grade, relocation of the meter set, and in locations where 
9 
neither of those solutions is possible, relocation of the meter set.  Buried vent lines 
10 
will be identified through the inside meter inspection program.  The capital forecast 
11 
for this program is approximately $0.1 million for KEDNY and $0.3 million for 
12 
KEDLI in each of the Rate Years and Data Years, adjusted for inflation.         
13 
14 
I.
Low Pressure Main Valve Installation Program
15 
Q.
Please describe the Low-Pressure Main Valve Installation Program. 
16 
A.
The Companies are proposing a safety enhancement program to install shut-off 
17 
valves approximately every 1,000 feet on low-pressure main in conjunction with the 
18 
Companies’ retirement of LPP.  The valves can be operated by first responders to 
19 
enable faster, and more effective reaction to an incident and are an important 
20 
component toward improving the overall safety of low-pressure systems.  During 
21 
3739


--- Page 50 ---
37 of 64 
2018, KEDNY conducted a pilot to test installation design for shut off valves 
1 
through installation of six shut-off valves on the low-pressure system between 64th 
2 
Street and 66th Street in Brooklyn.  Using lessons learned from the pilot program, 
3 
the Companies modified the installation design to reduce costs.  Based on the 
4 
Companies’ annual target mileage for LPP retirement, the program includes 
5 
installation of between 290 and 330 valves on KEDNY’s system each year between 
6 
CY 2020 and CY 2023, and approximately eleven valves per year on KEDLI’s 
7 
system during the same period.  The capital investment plan includes a forecast of 
8 
approximately $2.5 million for KEDNY and $0.05 million for KEDLI for each of 
9 
the Rate Year and Data Years, adjusted for inflation, respectively, and adjusted for 
10 
a slight increase in valve installations in the Data Years for KEDNY.  The costs for 
11 
the Low-Pressure Main Valve Installation Program are shown separately in Exhibit 
12 
__ (GIOP-1) for transparency, but will ultimately be accounted for in the total unit 
13 
costs of the various main and service replacement programs.          
14

K. Single Meter Regulator Inspections Program

The Companies currently follow an inspection program for regulators supplying 
15 
multi-meter headers (“multi-meter regulators”), but regulators supplying single 
16

17 
change for active services under the Companies’ meter test program, which is based 
18 
on a sample population of meters each year.  The Companies propose the Single 
19 
Meter Inspection Program to ensure that the entire single meter regulator population 
20 
is inspected at least once every 20 years, like multi-meter regulator inspections, to 
21 
3741


--- Page 52 ---
39 of 64 
ensure continued safe and reliable operation.  The program requires incremental 
1 
non-labor O&M to conduct additional inspections in the amount of approximately 
2 
$0.780 million for KEDNY and $4.000 million in KEDLI in each of the Rate Year 
3 
and Data Years, respectively.  Incremental customer meter services FTEs are also 
4 
required to perform this work.  KEDNY requires three additional technicians in 
5 
each of the Rate Year and Data Years, and KEDLI requires 24 additional 
6 
technicians, and one management supervisor in each of the Rate Year and Data 
7 
Years, due to the higher number of single meters and broader geographic area 
8 
covered by KEDLI’s service territory.

L. Gas Control SOP Training Program
11 
Q.
Please explain the Gas Control SOP Training Program. 
12 
A.
Similar to the I&R safety programs described above, this program directly responds 
13 
to recommendations coming out of the Merrimack Valley incident.  Following that 
14 
incident, Staff recommended that New York gas utilities develop job-specific 
15 
procedures for certain activities, including high/low pressure tie-ins, abandonment, 
16 
and regulator station operation verification. Staff further recommends that 
17 
employees and contractors should be trained on the new procedures, and that 
18 
training should include job-specific abnormal operating conditions and responsive 
19 
actions to be taken.  The Companies already had in place job-specific procedures, 
20 
known internally as System Operator Process (“SOP”) procedures, that covered 
21 
3742


--- Page 53 ---
40 of 64 
most areas of concern; however, the Companies made some changes and 
1 
improvements to its SOPs in light of the Companies’ engineering review and Staff’s 
2 
recommendations.  The result of the Companies’ SOP improvements is that more 
3 
field work is covered by SOPs, requiring increased training and field oversight.  
4 
5 
The Gas Control SOP Training Program is a further safety improvement to 
6 
implement recommended best practices, including supplemental field-based 
7 
training on SOPs and enhanced field QA/QC oversight of SOP execution and 
8 
inspection of work.  The proposal is for 2.3 Gas Control Center FTEs for KEDNY 
9 
and 2.2 Gas Control Center FTEs in KEDLI in each of the Rate Year and Data 
10 
Years to conduct field training and oversight.   
11

M. KEDLI Meter Pits Program
13 
Q.
Please explain the KEDLI Meter Pits Program. 
14 
A.
In KEDLI’s service territory, some customer meters are located in below-grade 
15 
meter pits that KEDLI has found can experience water intrusion and related 
16 
corrosion.  This program funds remediation of the entire population of meter pits. 
17 
KEDLI intends to remove meter pits and relocate meters to above-grade where 
18 
possible, and where meter relocation is not possible, KEDLI will address corrosion 
19 
issues and will make improvements to the meter pits to decrease the possibility of 
20 
water intrusion.  The Companies’ capital investment plan includes approximately 
21 
3743


--- Page 54 ---
41 of 64 
$1.1 million per year in each of the Rate Year and Data Years, adjusted for 
1 
inflation, for this program.

5

13

14 
designated first responders gas safety protocols so that they could then train others 
15 
in their departments.   
16 
17 
Similarly, Niagara Mohawk revised its field training curriculum, expanded the 
18

19 
to operator qualifications of contractors highlight the importance of robust training 
20 
3744

programs, particularly field training.  Field training involves assessing contractor 
1

•
Developing standardized assessment criteria;
3 
•
Identifying training program strengths and weaknesses;
4 
•
Evaluating each contractor training program to ensure that it is consistent
5 
with the Companies’ guidelines and that personnel have construction skills
6 
meeting National Grid’s standards;
7 
•
Continuously monitoring and evaluating each contractor’s training program
8 
to ensure corrective actions are effectively implemented;
9

Working as a partner with contractor trainers to share best practices and
10

11 
modules; and
12

Ensuring that contractor personnel operator qualifications are current.

The Companies propose to incorporate these improvements and add four First

O. Expanded Residential Methane Detection Program
4 
Q.
Please explain the Residential Methane Detection Program. 
5 
A.
In the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases, KEDNY implemented a Residential 
6 
Methane Detection Pilot Program to purchase and distribute commercially available 
7 
RMDs and gas safety brochures to customers living in apartments with inside 
8 
meters (room sets) when crews are on-site to perform mandated inside service line 
9 
inspections.  In CY 2018, Niagara Mohawk implemented a similar Residential 
10 
Methane Detection Program to distribute over 50,000 RMDs during service line 
11 
inspections and other customer interfacing opportunities (such as the New York 
12 
State Fair).  Niagara Mohawk’s program also included an enhanced customer 
13 
education component to develop a new and improved gas safety brochure for 
14 
distribution to customers along with the RMDs that includes information related to 
15 
use of RMDs and gas safety in general.  KEDNY is incorporating the new brochure 
16 
into its pilot program for CY 2019 and is continuing to make improvements to the 
17 
brochure to address additional topics including moving inside meters to outside.   
18 
19 
Both KEDNY’s and Niagara Mohawks’ Residential Methane Detection Programs 
20 
have been very well-received by customers.  RMDs are low cost, and easy-to-use 
21 
3746


--- Page 57 ---
44 of 64 
plug in devices, similar to smoke and carbon monoxide detectors, and are an 
1 
effective means to add a layer of protection against the risk of leaking gas.  The 
2 
program also has shown an ancillary benefit of encouraging customers to allow 
3 
access to inside facilities for required inspections.  For these reasons, the 
4 
Companies propose to expand the scope of KEDNY’s Residential Methane 
5 
Detection Program and to incorporate this program in KEDLI’s service territory.  In 
6 
particular, the Companies propose to distribute approximately 54,000 RMDs in 
7 
KEDNY’s service territory and approximately 36,000 RMDs in KEDLI’s service 
8 
territory, including enhancements to customer education and outreach, beginning in 
9 
the Rate Year and continuing through the Data Years.  The Companies intend to 
10 
continue to leverage the inside service inspections and will seek other customer 
11 
facing opportunities for distribution across all customer classes, and specifically 
12 
targeting room set meter customers, low income customers, multi-use residences, 
13 
and customers served by smaller diameter (two and four inch) cast iron piping. 
14 
This program requires incremental non-labor O&M of approximately $1.896 
15 
million for KEDNY and $1.264 million for KEDLI in each of the Rate Year and 
16 
Data Years, respectively.  As discussed above, while the costs for this program are 
17 
currently included in the revenue requirements, the Companies would be willing to 
18 
fund this program through the NRA deferrals to help mitigate the rate request in 
19 
these proceedings.             
20 
21 
3747


--- Page 58 ---
45 of 64 
P. Enhanced High Emitter Methane Detection Program
1 
Q.
Please explain the Enhanced High Emitter Methane Detection Program. 
2 
A.
In the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases, the Companies committed to 
3 
collaborating with the Environmental Defense Fund (“EDF”) to implement a 
4 
methane detection pilot program to gather and analyze leak flow data to prioritize 
5 
system investments and leak repairs.  During CYs 2017 and 2018, EDF conducted a 
6 
cavity ring-down spectroscopy (“CRDS”) pilot program using a Google Car 
7 
outfitted with CRDS technology to drive on various streets in KEDLI’s service 
8 
territory.  The results were analyzed by Colorado State University to attempt to 
9 
quantify detected leaks for methane emissions, and leaks characterized through this 
10 
methodology as high emitters were communicated to the Companies.  
11 
12 
During CY 2019, the Companies are continuing to assist Colorado State with its 
13 
next phase of analysis of the 2018 CRDS testing results.  The Companies are 
14 
providing access to and guidance on their gas system GIS mapping to enable the 
15 
University to conduct geospatial analysis.  The Companies will continue to support 
16 
these efforts and will consider the results and possible expanded application of the 
17 
CRDS technology.     
18 
19 
While those efforts are ongoing, the Companies propose an incremental O&M 
20 
program to continue to explore limited use of CRDS to enhance the leak repair 
21 
3748


--- Page 59 ---
46 of 64 
programs.  Specifically, the Companies are proposing to hire a contractor to operate 
1 
a Google car with CRDS technology for use in areas previously identified as 
2 
possible high leak concentration areas to aid in prioritizing for repair possible large 
3 
emitters within those areas.  This program requires incremental non-labor O&M in 
4 
the amount of approximately $0.250 million for KEDNY and $0.350 million for 
5 
KEDLI in each of the Rate Year and Data Years, respectively.  The program also 
6 
requires an incremental gas distribution engineer FTE, as shown on 
7 
Exhibit___(GIOP-9), to work with procurement and contracts strategy to develop a 
8 
contract for leasing the CRDS technology, manage the contractor, and monitor 
9 
progress, and analyze and report results.   
10 
11 
Q. Restrictions for Elevated Gas Infrastructure Program
12 
Q.
Please describe the Restrictions for Elevated Gas Infrastructure Program. 
13 
A.
This program would install fencing or other physical deterrents to restrict and deter 
14 
public access to elevated gas facilities (four feet or higher above the ground or 
15 
across a body of water).   The purpose of this program is to reduce the risk to the 
16 
public of climb and fall injuries or fatalities.  The program is in part in reaction to 
17 
an accident that occurred in the service territory of the Companies’ U.K. affiliate 
18 
involving a teenager who climbed an elevated gas facility and fell.  Niagara 
19 
Mohawk is currently undertaking a similar program that was approved in Niagara 
20 
Mohawk’s recent rate case.  The program requires capital investments of 
21 
3749


--- Page 60 ---
47 of 64 
approximately $0.4 million for KEDNY and $0.5 million for KEDLI in each of the 
1 
Rate Year and Data Years.          
2 
3 
III.
Gas Safety and Compliance Measures 
4 
Q.
Are the Companies proposing revisions to their current gas safety and 
5 
compliance measures?  
6 
A.
Yes.  The Companies’ gas safety and compliance measures were set in the 2016 
7 
KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases with performance targets established for CYs 
8 
2017, 2018, and 2019, continuing until modified by the Commission.  As discussed 
9 
below, the Companies propose revisions to: (i) establish more stretching 
10 
performance targets for certain metrics, such as KEDNY’s LPP retirement mileage 
11 
targets, that are challenging, but achievable, and will ensure the Companies are 
12 
improving their performance; (ii) simplify and streamline the mechanics of certain 
13 
metrics by collapsing categories and layers of measurement; (iii) reflect and reward 
14 
performance against metrics by redistributing potential NRAs among the various 
15 
performance measures while maintaining the same overall exposure; and (iv) align 
16 
metrics with those of other utilities, including Orange & Rockland (“O&R”) and 
17 
Niagara Mohawk.  The Companies also propose limited incentives to encourage 
18 
outperformance and productivity improvements.   
19 
20 
3750


--- Page 61 ---
48 of 64 
It should be noted that the gas safety and compliance measures are measured on a 
1 
CY basis.  As such, references to adjustments in performance targets or 
2 
implementation of new metrics and incentives are proposed to begin January 1, 
3 
2020.  
4 
5 
Q.
Please summarize the Companies’ proposed gas safety and compliance 
6 
measure proposal. 
7 
A.
To continue to encourage performance against targets, the Companies propose to 
8 
maintain the existing cap of 150 pre-tax basis points at risk per year for failure to 
9 
meet targets that was established in the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases.  To 
10 
more fairly allocate the 150 at risk basis points across the categories of performance 
11 
targets, the Companies propose reducing the total at risk for gas regulations 
12 
performance measure from 100 basis points per year to 75 basis points per year, and 
13 
distributing the 25 basis points among the other performance measure categories. 
14 
This approach is aligned with the approach recently adopted by the Commission for 
15 
O&R’s safety and compliance performance measures.  The Companies’ proposal is 
16 
illustrated in Table 2.  Specific targets and measures for each category are set forth 
17 
in more detail below. 
18 
19

13

5 
Q.
Please summarize the Companies’ LPP proposal.  
6 
A.
Overall, the Companies’ LPP proposal ensures they collectively will replace at least 
7 
215 miles of LPP each year and a cumulative 930 miles over four years (with an 
8 
associated negative revenue adjustment for failing to achieve the targets), while 
9 
providing incentives to accelerate LPP replacement each year. The proposal 
10 
provides flexibility to target additional replacements when resources are available 
11 
and other opportunities present to complete the work more cost effectively.  The 
12 
surcharge mechanism ensures that KEDNY and KEDLI will recover LPP 
13 
replacement costs only to the extent it is successful in delivering its program.

8

E. Gas Regulations Performance Measure
3 
Q.
Please describe how the Companies currently measure performance against 
4 
safety regulations. 
5 
A.
KEDNY and KEDLI are currently subject to a Gas Safety Regulations Performance 
6 
Metric that exposes the Companies to significant NRAs (up to 100 pre-tax basis 
7 
points) based on violations of certain Commission’s rules and regulations contained 
8 
in 16 NYCRR Parts 255 (Transmission and Distribution of Gas) and 261 (Piping 
9 
Beyond the Meter) that are set forth in Appendix 9 of the 2016 KEDNY and 
10 
KEDLI Rate Case Joint Proposal, as determined by Staff’s annual field and records 
11 
3764


--- Page 75 ---
62 of 64 
audits.  Specifically, the metric imposes a NRA for each occurrence of a violation 
1 
identified during these audits in accordance with specific targets. The number of 
2 
occurrences of non-compliance with each “high risk” and “other risk” regulation is 
3 
capped at ten per year.  
4 
5 
Q.
What are the Companies’ proposals regarding the Gas Regulations 
6 
Performance Measure?  
7 
A.
KEDNY and KEDLI take compliance with the Commission’s regulations, 
8 
especially regulations affecting the safe operation of the Companies’ gas system, 
9 
very seriously.  The Companies also acknowledge that the cap of ten occurrences 
10 
per year of non-compliance with each regulation that was put in place in the 2016 
11 
KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases helped to alleviate the overly punitive nature of 
12 
the prior metric that allowed for unlimited occurrences.  However, based on the 
13 
Companies record of improvement in compliance since 2015, the Companies 
14 
believe that a further adjustment to the targets is appropriate.  In particular, the 
15 
Companies propose that the NRAs be spread between larger tiers, in line with the 
16 
structure that was approved for Niagara Mohawk.  The Companies also propose 
17 
that the total NRA per year be capped at 75 basis points.  The Companies’ proposal 
18 
is reflected in Table 10.   
19 
20 
21 
3765


--- Page 76 ---
63 of 64

I. 
Introduction 
1 
Q.
Please introduce the members of the Gas Safety Panel. 
2 
A.
The Panel consists of Ross W. Turrini, Timothy S. Graham, and Annette Saxman. 
3 
4 
Q.
Is this the same Panel that previously submitted direct testimony in these 
5 
proceedings? 
6 
A.
Yes.  The terms defined in the Panel’s direct testimony have the same meanings here.   
7 
8 
Q.
What is the purpose of the Panel’s corrections and updates testimony? 
9 
A.
The purpose of the Panel’s corrections and updates testimony is to identify and explain the 
10 
updates and corrections to the capital investment and/or O&M forecasts for the Inside 
11 
Service Line Inspections Program, the Enhanced Inactive Accounts Program, and the I&R 
12 
Distribution Station Overpressure Protection and Control Line Mapping Programs. 
13 
Updates to the forecasts for these programs are described below and are included in 
14 
KEDNY and KEDLI’s respective Exhibits __ (GIOP-1CU) (updated capital investment 
15 
plan), KEDNY Exhibit __ (GIOP-8CU) and KEDLI Exhibit __ (GIOP-7CU) (updated 
16 
incremental O&M expenditures), and KEDNY Exhibit __ (GIOP-9CU) and KEDLI 
17 
Exhibit __ (GIOP-8CU) (updated incremental FTE positions).  These exhibits were 
18 
prepared by or under the supervision and direction of one or more members of the Panel.

5

6

7

8

9

13

14 
to shorten the end-to-end process for addressing inactive accounts to a 45-day process by 
15 
the end of the Rate Year and to 30 days by the end of Data Year 2.  The Companies re-
16

17 
KEDNY’s initial forecast of incremental FTEs was overstated.  Specifically, KEDNY’s 
18

19

20

Schedule 27, Workpaper 2.

I&R Distribution Station Overpressure Protection and Control Line Survey
9 
and GPS Mapping Programs
10 
Q.
Please describe the corrections to the forecast for the I&R Distribution Station 
11 
Overpressure Protection and Control Line Survey and GPS Mapping Programs. 
12 
A.
The Companies inadvertently excluded costs for these two programs related to enhancing 
13 
overpressure protection and safety at distribution stations.  First, KEDLI’s capital forecast 
14 
for the Distribution Over Pressure Protection Program did not include the costs of adding 
15 
relief valves at distribution stations of approximately $1.2 million in each of the Rate Year, 
16 
Data Year 1, and Data Year 2.  There are no costs added for Data Year 3 because relief 
17 
valve installation will be complete by the end of Data Year 2.  Installation of relief valves 
18 
will serve as an additional layer of overpressure protection for distribution stations.    
19 
20 
Second, KEDNY and KEDLI’s non-labor O&M forecasts for the Control Line Survey and 
21 
GPS Mapping programs are being adjusted to include the costs of reviewing as-built 
22 
transmission station drawings against the physical facilities to ensure the accuracy of the 
23 
3772


--- Page 83 ---

Page 4 of 5 
station documentation in coordination with survey and mapping of the control lines.  The 
1

2 
$0.7 for KEDNY and KEDLI, respectively, in each of the Rate Year and Data Years 1 and 
3 
2. There are no costs in Data Year 3 because the document review will be complete by the
4 
end of Data Year 2.     
5 
6 
Both programs are important components of the overall I&R gas safety program 
7 
enhancements that will improve the Companies’ ability to respond to abnormal operating 
8 
conditions, such as an over pressurization.  As discussed in the Panel’s direct testimony, 
9

III. 
Gas Safety Measures Performance Targets and Incentives
1 
A. Goals of Gas Safety Performance Targets and Incentives
2 
Q.
What is the purpose of gas safety metrics and performance targets? 
3 
A.
The Companies’ current rate plans establish certain metrics and performance 
4 
targets to ensure that the Companies meet their regulatory requirements to 
5 
provide safe and reliable service to customers.  The metrics measure the 
6 
Companies’ performance across various activities, ranging from LPP removal 
7 
to emergency response, and are designed to ensure accountability to customers. 
8 
Metrics may include positive revenue adjustments (“PRAs”) or NRAs if the 
9 
Companies’ performance varies from established levels.  NRAs serve to 
10 
penalize the Companies for underperformance, while PRAs incent the 
11 
Companies to overachieve base expectations and deliver incremental 
12 
improvements.  Inclusion of corresponding adjustments is important to drive 
13 
performance and support public policy objectives. 
14 
15 
Q.
Please explain why inclusion of PRAs and incentives are appropriate. 
16 
A.
PRAs do not simply reward the Companies for basic operations or business as 
17 
usual activities.  PRAs incentivize the Companies to perform additional work 
18 
and provide enhanced services, whether it be incremental leak repair or LPP 
19 
removal, that ultimately benefit customers and the community and further align 
20 
the Companies’ performance with public policy objectives.  Appropriately 
21 
3789


--- Page 100 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of Gas Safety Panel 
Page 14 of 64 
challenging PRAs support continuous improvement and superior performance 
1 
with the goal of exceeding customer expectations and industry standards. 
2 
3 
Q.
What is the Companies’ position on the SPP’s proposal for an overall 
4 
safety and reliability capital investment productivity metric in lieu of the 
5 
LPP unit cost productivity incentive? 
6 
A.
The Companies believe the LPP unit cost productivity incentive is an 
7 
appropriate mechanism to encourage better cost management for the proactive 
8 
LPP retirement program, assuming the targets are set appropriately based on 
9 
current unit costs.  The Companies are interested in the SPP’s proposal for a 
10 
broader capital program metric that could complement the existing net plant 
11 
tracker.  The Companies do not agree that the LPP productivity incentive should 
12 
be removed; an overall capital program productivity metric could be designed 
13 
to exclude the LPP retirement program productivity to avoid any overlap.   
14 
15 
Q.
Are metrics appropriate for a one-year rate plan? 
16 
A. 
No.  The gas safety performance metrics are designed to drive performance
17 
improvements over time.  To that end, the Companies’ proposals for 
18 
performance metrics contemplate a multi-year rate plan.  The Companies do not 
19 
agree with the inclusion of performance metrics in a one-year litigated case. 
20 
Indeed, the Companies submit that their consistently high level of performance 
21 
3790


--- Page 101 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of Gas Safety Panel 
Page 15 of 64 
should provide the Commission with sufficient comfort that service will not be 
1

C. Use of NRA Deferral Balances to Fund Safety Programs
6 
Q.
Please explain the Companies’ NRA deferral balances. 
7 
A.
The Companies’ current rate plans contain several gas safety metrics to ensure 
8 
the provision of safe and reliable service to customers.  The Companies’ 
9 
performance relative to those metrics is tracked annually, and failure to meet 
10 
the metrics can result in NRAs, which are deferred for future customer benefit.  
11 
12 
Q.
How have the Companies historically used NRA deferral balances? 
13 
A.
The Companies have historically used NRA balances to fund designated safety 
14 
programs for the benefit of customers.  Such treatment is consistent with how 
15 
other utilities across New York State, including Consolidated Edison and 
16 
Niagara Mohawk, have used such deferral balances.  In the 2016 KEDNY and 
17 
KEDLI Rate Cases, the Joint Proposal approved by the Commission 
18 
specifically provides that “[a]ny Gas Safety Performance Metrics negative 
19 
revenue adjustments incurred by KEDNY [and KEDLI] will be deferred for 
20 
future use in incremental safety programs.”  For example, Niagara Mohawk is 
21 
3792


--- Page 103 ---

currently using NRA deferral balances to fund its Residential Methane 
1 
Detection program and for GPS mapping of gas facilities.

Q.
What are the SPSP’s recommendations for the use of KEDNY and 
4 
KEDLI’s NRA deferral balances? 
5 
A.
The SPSP recommends that the Companies use the NRA deferral balances to 
6 
fund the Companies’ proposed Expanded Residential Methane Detector, I&R 
7

10

11

Program and the Incremental I&R Control Line Survey and GPS Program.  The

Program separately below.   
19 
20 
3793

Rebuttal Testimony of Gas Safety Panel

The NYCGISP testimony also appears to draw a negative inference from the

A. Leak Prone Pipe Retirement
14 
i. LPP Targets and Prioritization
15 
Q.
Does the SPSP agree with the Companies’ proposed annual LPP 
16 
retirement targets and prioritization methodology for retirement of LPP? 
17 
A.
Yes, the SPSP supports KEDNY’s proposal to continue its five-mile per year 
18

19

20

21 
3795


--- Page 106 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of Gas Safety Panel 
Page 20 of 64

1

2 
and is aligned with the LPP retirement target metric in place for the Companies’ 
3 
upstate affiliate, Niagara Mohawk.   
4 
5 
Moreover, the SPSP supports KEDNY’s risk-based method for prioritizing the 
6 
highest-risk LPP for retirement based on multiple risk factors. (SPSP at 15-16). 
7 
8 
Q.
Please discuss NYC’s recommendation that the Companies accelerate 
9 
retirement of low pressure LPP that is located in FEMA 500 and 100-year 
10 
floodplains. 
11 
A.
NYC recommends that the Companies be required to replace all LPP located in 
12 
FEMA 500 and 100-year floodplains within 10 years.  While NYC expresses 
13 
opposition to incentives for all other gas safety metrics, including incentives for 
14 
the general retirement of additional LPP, NYC proposes an incentive to 
15 
encourage accelerated LPP retirement in floodplains.  (NYCGISP at 25-26). 
16 
17 
Q.
Do the Companies currently replace LPP in floodplains?   
18 
A.
Yes, as NYC notes, the Companies have made a modification to their main 
19 
prioritization algorithm, procedure ENG04030 “Identification, Evaluation and 
20 
Prioritization of Distribution Main Segments for Replacement” that assigns a 
21 
3796


--- Page 107 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of Gas Safety Panel 
Page 21 of 64 
higher weight to LPP mains in floodplains.  This will help replace the LPP 
1 
mains in floodplains earlier but will not guarantee completion of these 
2 
replacements in ten years.  In addition, the Companies have identified several 
3 
pockets of low-pressure LPP in floodplains that the Companies intend to replace 
4 
to uprate the network to high pressure over the next five years.  The Companies 
5 
also note that all of the LPP and low-pressure main has been replaced west of 
6 
Bay 32nd Street in the Rockaways, which was an area hit particularly hard 
7 
during Superstorm Sandy.  Therefore, the Companies maintain that increased 
8 
acceleration to replace LPP in floodplains is unnecessary.   
9 
10 
Q.
How would further acceleration of LPP retirement in the floodplains 
11 
impact the Companies’ proactive LPP retirement program? 
12 
A.
To specifically target and accelerate more retirements in the floodplains, the 
13 
Companies may need to defer or delay the replacement of higher-risk pipe in 
14 
other regions.  This is not recommended.  The Companies’ acknowledge NYC’s 
15 
concern that water intrusion caused by flooding can result in shutoffs and 
16 
relights that can take time (NYCGISP at 23); however, the risks of possible 
17 
failures on riskier segments of pipe experiencing condition issues, or of older 
18 
vintages than those in floodplains, outweigh re-light concerns.  Moreover, the 
19 
Companies’ program to install automatic flood-sensing shutoff valves in the 
20 
floodplains over the next several years will provide instant information of the 
21 
3797


--- Page 108 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of Gas Safety Panel 
Page 22 of 64 
number of customers impacted by flooding that can help inform and make more 
1 
efficient the Companies’ service restoration and re-light processes.   
2 
3 
In short, the Companies believe that their existing risk-based prioritization 
4 
model is the best approach for meeting the goal of retiring all LPP within 
5 
floodplains.   
6 
7 
Q.
Is NYC correct in its belief that low pressure LPP retirement in the 
8 
floodplains could be accelerated and replaced within 10 years at no 
9 
additional cost? 
10 
A.
No.  NYC reasons incorrectly that the pace of replacements in the floodplains 
11 
over the past several years would be representative and that the Companies 
12 
would therefore easily meet NYC’s targets within the proposed program 
13 
budgets.  First, some of the Companies’ low-pressure main that is located in 
14 
floodplains are part of extensive low-pressure networks that have no high-
15 
pressure mains nearby to facilitate easy conversion and, therefore, would be 
16 
challenging to address over a shorter period of time from a resource perspective. 
17 
Second, because the Companies’ prioritization model is risk-based and the 
18 
work plan is established each year for the following year, there is no guarantee 
19 
that the historic pattern of replacements in floodplains will repeat in future 
20 
periods.  Riskier segments may be more concentrated outside the floodplain in 
21 
3798


--- Page 109 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of Gas Safety Panel 
Page 23 of 64 
any given year depending on the totality of circumstances.  The Companies 
1 
would lose efficiencies of coordinating LPP replacements with municipal 
2 
projects and other company work across the Companies’ territory if they were 
3 
required to target floodplain work over other work, which could increase costs. 
4 
Third, because of the volume of work that would need to be done in these more 
5 
concentrated areas, municipal permitting stipulations could slow the pace of the 
6 
work and require additional resources to enable the Companies to meet targets. 
7 
For these reasons, the Companies believe that NYC’s proposal would require 
8 
incremental resources.

3801

(SPSP at 28-30). 
3 
4 
Q.
Do the Companies agree with the SPSP’s proposals? 
5 
A.
No.  The Companies believe the Leak Backlog Metrics they have proposed are 
6

7 
backlog while strategically targeting the highest emission Type-3 leaks to 
8

9 
respond specifically as follows: 
10 
•
Baseline targets:  The current Leak Backlog metric reduces the targets
11

12 
It is reasonable, therefore, to set CY 2020 targets based on a 150 and
13 
750 reduction from the 2019 targets as the Companies have proposed.
14 
Leak repair is reactive and unpredictable.  Even as LPP is replaced with
15 
new main, the LPP that remains on the system is aging, and leaks are
16 
continuing to rise in KEDNY’s service territory.  The Companies’
17 
proposal is appropriately challenging, especially given the additional
18 
downward adjustment annually based on high-emitter Type-3 leak
19

20 
3803


--- Page 114 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of Gas Safety Panel 
Page 28 of 64 
•
Annual reduction:  The Companies support a fixed annual reduction.
1 
The proposal to further reduce the targets each year based on high-
2 
emitter Type-3 leak repairs was intended to work in conjunction with
3 
the Companies’ proposed incentive structure, so that as the Companies
4 
earned incentives for repairing high-emitter Type-3 leaks, the metric
5 
would become more challenging the following year.
6 
•
Workable leaks:  Past year-end backlogs are not necessarily
7 
representative of workable leaks that might arise.  Workable leaks
8 
typically require more time and resources to repair, and, if too many
9 
arose near the end of the year, the Companies could struggle to meet a
10 
lower metric.  The existing target of 25 leaks per year is challenging and
11 
reasonable.
12 
•
Year-end target range:  The SPSP gives no justification for reducing the
13 
Companies’ proposed 11-day target window to a 6-day target window.
14 
Again, new leaks can arise at any time and increase as the weather
15 
becomes colder.  The Companies’ leak repair performance could be
16 
excellent for nearly a year, yet the Companies would be at risk of
17 
missing their targets if too many leaks arise in the last month of the year
18 
without sufficient time to make repairs.  Moreover, the Commission
19 
accepted a December 21st – December 31st target period recently for
20 
3804


--- Page 115 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of Gas Safety Panel 
Page 29 of 64 
Orange and Rockland Utilities, Inc. (“Orange and Rockland”) in Case 
1 
18-G-0068.
2 
•
Incentives:  The Companies strongly believe that the Leak Backlog
3

4

5

12

reduction metric.
15 
16

in the metric? 
18 
A.
No.  As the SPSP notes in its testimony, although 16 NYCRR §255.819(a)

21 
3805

this requirement would require the Companies to add considerable O&M

3 
Requiring deployment of crews back to every Type-3 leak repair adds costs

without commensurate benefits.     
5 
6 
Q.
Do the Companies agree with NYC’s proposal to eliminate the entire leak 
7 
backlog in five years? 
8 
A.
No.  The Commission’s safety regulations include deadlines for repairing Type

16 
repair of non-hazardous leaks.  Yet NYC proposes acceleration of leak repair

19 
20 
3806

Rebuttal Testimony of Gas Safety Panel

1

2 
A.
EDF’s proposal is to change fundamentally the structure of the leak 
3

4

5

11

(EDF at 30-35).

Q.
Do the Companies agree with EDF’s proposal?

A.

20 
3807

The Companies maintain, however, that the advanced leak detection technology 
1 
is not yet sufficiently reliable to form the basis for a new metric for reasons that 
2 
are discussed in further detail in section B(2) below.  The Companies’ agree 
3 
that they should begin to integrate CRDS technology into their arsenal of tools 
4 
to combat methane emission, via their proposed Enhanced High Emitter 
5 
Methane Detection Program.  This program provides for deployment of Google 
6 
car-mounted CRDS technology operated by contractors in each of KEDNY’s 
7 
and KEDLI’s service territories to prioritize high emission leaks for repair in 
8 
limited areas already know to have a high leak concentration.  Even if the 
9 
Companies believed that the technology could produce a reliable baseline 
10 
measure, to achieve a full baseline survey of the Companies entire service areas 
11 
that EDF proposes would require significantly more contractor resources to 
12 
cover each territory and to conduct multiple passes required to obtain a 
13 
sufficient data set, plus additional incremental Company full-time equivalents 
14 
(“FTEs”) to process and analyze data, and manage the program.  Given all 
15 
factors including the limitations of the technology and costs, the Companies 
16 
believe a more measured approach during the Rate Year and Data Years is more 
17 
reasonable than rushing toward a new metric.  The Companies’ proposed 
18 
Enhanced High Emitter Methane Detection Program supports use of the Google 
19 
Car/CRDS technology in a more reasonable way and can provide an 
20 
opportunity to further evaluate this technology.

Page 37 of 64 
Additionally, the Companies should receive credit for relocates because 
1 
relocates are treated like a new ticket for purposes of Companies’ responses (as 
2 
if the first ticket was never issued).  Indeed, relocates are generated by the same 
3

4

5 
System, a “relocate” occurs when the One-Call Center issues a ticket and 
6

7 
excavator contacts the One-Call Center to issue a second ticket, or a “relocate,” 
8

9

10 
beyond the ten-day limit) or conditions such as weather or construction cause 
11 
the original markout to fade or disappear).  Although the ticket has the same 
12 
requesting party and location, this is not a “retransmit” because KEDNY and 
13 
KEDLI must respond again and relocate and re-mark their facilities as if they 
14 
had not been located and marked in the first instance.  Indeed, from a safety 
15 
standpoint, treating relocates as new tickets is a best practice, and exclusion of 
16 
these from the metrics could create a perverse incentive for the Companies to 
17 
focus less on relocate responses.  Because the Companies’ response to relocates 
18 
is the same as for all other tickets, and, to encourage further improvements in 
19 
damage prevention, they should continue to be counted in the Companies’ 
20 
metric.

D. Enhanced Inactive Accounts Program

11

12 
As was stated in the Panel’s direct testimony, under the Companies’ current 
13 
inactive accounts process, the Companies act immediately to perform a site 
14 
analysis on every site to determine whether conditions, such as a vacant premises 
15 
or other observable unsafe condition, warrant immediately securing the line.  In 
16 
such instances, the service is cut within 24 hours.  Assuming there is not an 
17 
urgent circumstance, the process is designed to resolve inactive accounts within 
18 
60 days.  In practice, however, 47 percent of KEDNY’s inactive accounts and 
19 
89 percent of KEDLI’s inactive accounts are resolved within the first 15 days, 
20

21 
3824


--- Page 135 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of Gas Safety Panel 
Page 49 of 64 
accounts remain inactive between days 45 and 60 of the current process.  The 
1 
Companies’ proposal to achieve a 30-day process by the end of Data Year 2 will 
2 
expedite the entire process.     
3 
4 
Regarding meter locations, at the time of original installation, the Companies 
5 
install meters in readily accessible locations (i.e., locations not obstructed and 
6 
able to be accessed to perform inspections and maintenance), regardless of 
7 
whether the meter is physically located on the outside or inside of a customer’s 
8 
building.  Over the past several years, the Companies have discovered meters 
9 
that are obstructed or inaccessible because of subsequent customer alterations or 
10 
obstructions installed around the meter.  The Companies’ current practice is to 
11 
advise customers of obstructions and access issues upon discovery.  If required 
12 
to resolve an imminent safety concern, the Companies would terminate service.   
13 
Outside of imminent safety conditions, however, the Companies have 
14 
encountered several impediments that prevent or delay immediate removal of 
15 
the obstructions, including customer refusals, local building codes and 
16 
regulations, exterior or interior obstacles, and other various safety reasons. 
17 
These challenges are further compounded in the City of New York, where 
18 
KEDNY has been unable to relocate any meters outside of the building because 
19 
of code interpretation issues with the NYC Department of Buildings (“DOB”). 
20 
Accordingly, although the Companies will continue their efforts to remove 
21 
3825


--- Page 136 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of Gas Safety Panel 
Page 50 of 64 
obstructions and overcome the impediments noted above as quickly and 
1 
efficiently as possible, Staff’s recommendation that the Companies take 
2 
“immediate and continuous” action to remedy these conditions is simply 
3 
unachievable for reasons beyond the Companies’ control.  The Companies 
4 
acknowledge that improvements in this area will require the involvement of third 
5 
parties, including municipalities, and invite affected stakeholders to provide 
6 
comments and work with the Companies towards effective resolutions.    
7

9 
Q. 
What is the SGIOP’s recommendation regarding the Companies’ proposed
10

11 
A.

12 
from the Companies’ capital plans and should instead be funded by the 
13 
Companies’ shareholders because the SGIOP believes the Companies should 
14 
already have addressed buried vent lines.  (SGIOP at 95).

17 
A. 
No.  The Companies have identified and addressed buried vent lines for years
18

19

20 
meter changes, and procedures for ensuring the safe operation of buried vent 
21 
3826

lines and performing corrective actions when necessary are included in the

3

4

5 
This program is properly funded through rates.
