F. Low Pressure Main Valve Installation Program
8 
Q. 
Do the Companies agree with the SGIOP’s proposal for the Low-Pressure
9 
Main Valves Installation Program to be funded through NRAs rather than 
10 
through rates? 
11 
A. 
No.  This program is an incremental safety program that will be ongoing because
12 
the valves will be installed on new services as well as on existing main.  This is 
13 
an ongoing program with long-term benefits to customers that justify inclusion 
14 
in rates.  There will also be future investment required to ensure the valves 
15 
remain operational over time.  The costs of this program and the nature of this 
16 
program render it inappropriate to fund through the limited pool of NRA dollars 
17 
that are available.  This program should be funded through rates, and NRAs 
18 
should be reserved for shorter-term programs that do not require ongoing 
19 
spending.

G. Expanded Residential Methane Detector (“RMD”) Program
1 
Q. 
Please respond to the SPSP’s and NYC’s recommendations that the
2 
Companies consider development of advanced RMDs. 
3 
A. 
The Companies support the development of utility detection devices with
4 
advanced technologies for residential methane detection, similar to the Con 
5 
Edison device, that can automatically communicate information, including 
6 
methane emissions, to the Companies’ systems.  The Companies note that Con 
7

8 
that there is no device currently on the market that would be compatible. 
9 
Notwithstanding, the Companies have ongoing research and development 
10 
(“R&D”) efforts underway and actively communicate with all major 
11 
manufacturers regarding potential development of a product that meets the 
12 
Companies’ technical specifications.   
13 
14 
The Companies also support the advancement and improvement of RMDs for 
15 
customers’ personal use, similar to smoke detectors, of the type that the 
16 
Companies plan to distribute via the proposed Expanded Residential Methane 
17 
Detection Program.  The Companies’ efforts are described in their responses to 
18 
IR DPS-671, a copy of which is included in Exhibit __(GSP-1R), and in 
19 
KEDLI’s Settlement Agreement Implementation Update report that was filed on 
20 
August 22, 2019, in Cases 14-G-0058 and 14-G-0060. 
21 
3828


--- Page 139 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of Gas Safety Panel 
Page 53 of 64 
  The Companies intend to continue these efforts during the Rate Year and Data 
1 
Years.  The Companies note that Staff’s proposed reduction of the Companies’ 
2 
incremental FTE to support R&D efforts that is noted in the GIOP’s rebuttal 
3 
testimony is incompatible with the SPSP’s recommendation to dedicate 
4 
additional R&D resources toward the advancement of RMDs. 
5 
6 
Q. 
Do the Companies agree that NYC’s recommendations that the Companies
7

8

Program? 
10 
A.

11

12

13 
customers to install and to operate, similar to common smoke detectors and

15 
is shown response to IR CNY-75, a copy of which is included in Exhibit 
16

17 
been provided with RMDs, and there have thus far been no odor calls that have 
18 
originated from a Niagara Mohawk customer who was provided with a device. 
19 
The Companies intend to similarly track which customers receive RMDs and 
20 
can use this data in conjunction with leak calls to track whether leak calls are 
21 
3829


--- Page 140 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of Gas Safety Panel 
Page 54 of 64 
reported from customers who have an RMD device.  As noted in the response, 
1

2

3

21 
3830

The program is not a leak detection program but is a supplement to the 
1

3

1 
A. Gas Safety Reporting
2 
Q.

11 
12 
B. Local Law 152
13 
Q.
Does the SPSP discuss Local Law 152 (“LL152”)? 
14 
A. 
Yes.  As stated by the SPSP, LL152 is a New York City Council law that requires
15 
building owners in New York City to conduct inspections including atmospheric 
16 
corrosion and leak surveys of the natural gas piping inside buildings, including 
17 
the same segments that the Companies are required to inspect pursuant the inside 
18 
service line definition change.  (SPSP at 76).   
19 
20 
3832


--- Page 143 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of Gas Safety Panel 
Page 57 of 64 
The SPSP is correct that New York gas LDCs had hoped to coordinate LL152 
1 
inspections with utility inside service line inspections to eliminate overlapping 
2 
inspections of the same facilities and reduce the burden and costs for both 
3 
building owners and utility customers.  The Companies, Con Edison, and the 
4 
NGA worked with the DOB for nearly a year prior to issuance of the proposed 
5 
rules implementing the law to provide guidance and information on how 
6 
coordination could be achieved.  When the DOB issued its proposed rules on 
7 
November 30, 2018, the Companies were disappointed that the rules were not 
8 
written in a manner that would allow for coordination of inspections to meet 
9 
both the utilities and LL152 requirements, and the Companies filed joint 
10 
comments with Con Edison in the rulemaking proceeding. 
11 
12 
Q. 
What are the SPSP’s recommendations regarding LL152?
13 
A. 
The SPSP has three recommendations: (i) that the Companies continue to
14 
coordinate with DOB to encourage modifications to the rule that would enable 
15 
coordinated inspections; (ii) if the rules are issued without modification, that the 
16 
Companies file a plan within 60 days of the issuance of DOB’s final rule or 
17 
within 60 days of the Commission’s Order in these proceedings detailing how 
18 
the Companies will coordinate with building owners to encourage them to use 
19 
plumbers qualified to perform the utility’s inspections, including technology 
20 
sharing and an educational campaign; and (iii) that the amounts the Companies 
21 
3710
3833


--- Page 144 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of Gas Safety Panel 
Page 58 of 64 
can collect in rates should be a “placeholder” with a reconciliation to a new 
1 
estimate once the final rules are issued.   
2 
3 
Q. 
Please comment on the SPSP’s recommendations.
4 
A. 
The Companies are willing to continue to work with DOB to attempt to align
5 
LL152 with the utilities’ service line inspection requirements; however, it should 
6 
not be assumed that these efforts will be successful.  The Companies do not 
7 
agree with the remaining recommendations.  The SPSP’s recommendations 
8 
hinge on the concept that building owners, if properly educated and provided 
9 
with data recording tools, could voluntarily hire plumbers qualified to conduct 
10 
the inspections on behalf of the utilities so that the utilities could count the 
11 
inspections as completed for their regulatory obligations.  The misalignment of 
12 
the inspection cycle times (LL152 rules are every five years but the utilities are 
13 
one- and three-year cycles), however, and the borough-by-borough approach in 
14 
the LL152 rules as proposed, would render even voluntary coordination 
15 
impossible.  There are also other impediments caused by the rules as proposed 
16 
including the lack of a requirement to inspect room set meter locations.  Even if 
17 
the cycle times aligned, it would be difficult for the Companies to manage their 
18 
compliance program, resource needs, etc. around an unknown portion of 
19 
inspections occurring voluntarily in any given year.  The Companies remain 
20 
hopeful that their comments in the rulemaking proceeding will be given due 
21 
3834


--- Page 145 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of Gas Safety Panel 
Page 59 of 64 
consideration and that the final rules adopted will allow for coordination; 
1 
however, in the event they are passed as proposed, the Companies strongly 
2 
believe that the most cost-efficient way to proceed for the Companies’ customers 
3 
is for the Companies to manage their required inside service line inspections 
4 
independently.   
5 
6 
Q. 
Do the Companies agree that the amounts collected in rates for inside
7 
service line inspections should be temporary pending issuance of DOB’s 
8 
final rules? 
9 
A. 
No.  The Companies’ Inside Service Line Inspection Program for the Rate Year
10 
will be conducted independently by the Companies.  In the event that the LL152 
11 
rules are passed with sufficient modifications to allow for coordination of 
12 
inspections, it will take the Companies time to develop coordination plans that 
13 
ensure the Companies can meet their regulatory requirements.  The Companies 
14 
would not anticipate realization of coordination benefits in the Rate Year, but 
15 
this could be covered by an exogenous cost provision.       
16 
17 
C. Inside Meter Relocations
18 
Q. 
Please explain the inside meter relocation practices that were established in
19 
the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases and the SPSP’s recommendations 
20 
regarding relocation of inside meters going forward. 
21 
3835


--- Page 146 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of Gas Safety Panel 
Page 60 of 64 
A. 
Pursuant to the Joint Proposal adopted in the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate
1 
Case, beginning in CY 2018, the Companies were to begin to relocate gas meters 
2 
located inside premises to outside the premises when replacing or relocating gas 
3 
service lines as part of the LPP retirement program, except where (i) the 
4 
customer refuses permission to relocate the meter outside; (ii) local building 
5 
codes or similar regulations prohibit outside meters; (iii) exterior or interior 
6 
obstacles prevent meter relocation; or (iv) the gas meters should not be moved 
7 
outside for safety reasons.  The SPSP recommends that the Companies continue 
8 
to relocate inside meters to outside and expand their efforts to perform this work 
9 
not only in coordination with the LPP replacement programs, but also whenever 
10 
the Companies perform service transfers or move an existing service line to a 
11 
newly installed pipeline.  (SPSP at 82). 
12 
13 
Q. 
Can the Companies support continued and/or expanded inside meter
14 
relocations?   
15 
A. 
The Companies agree with the safety benefits of relocating meters from inside
16 
to outside.  Meter relocations would also eventually reduce costs of performing 
17 
required inspections of inside service lines.  At this time, however, KEDNY is 
18 
not able to make specific commitments regarding relocating meters from inside 
19 
to outside for reasons that are explained in more detail below.  KEDLI supports 
20 
continued inside meter relocations in coordination with the LPP replacement 
21 
3836


--- Page 147 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of Gas Safety Panel 
Page 61 of 64 
program, assuming the existing exceptions would still apply.  In the event of a 
1 
multi-year rate plan, KEDLI is open to considering expansion of these efforts to 
2 
other work where KEDLI is replacing the service from the building to the curb.  
3 
KEDLI notes that it would not be appropriate to require meter relocations to be 
4 
coordinated with service replacement work from the main to the curb.     
5 
6 
Q. 
Please explain why KEDNY is unable to commit to meter relocations at this
7 
time. 
8 
A. 
KEDNY’s meter relocation strategy assumed that it could perform this work
9 
using its crews that were on-site performing the main and service replacements 
10 
using its standard processes and procedures for replacing meters.  This is the 
11 
process followed by KEDLI in its service territory.  Early in CY 2018, KEDNY 
12 
became aware that the DOB considered part of the work that is required to 
13 
relocate the meters–specifically, the replacement of a small section of pipe inside 
14 
the premises where the meter was located to the new connection with the meter 
15 
outside the building–to be interior house pipe work falling under the 
16 
requirements of the New York City Building Code and Fuel Gas Code 
17 
(“Codes”).  These requirements are varied and complex; some examples include: 
18 
•
three-pound pressure testing beyond the installation to the entire
19 
premises that could compromise internal piping that otherwise
20 
3837


--- Page 148 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of Gas Safety Panel 
Page 62 of 64 
operates safely at lower usage pressures, particularly on older 
1 
buildings common in NYC ; 
2 
•
multiple inspections by DOB including a close out inspection such
3 
that the work could not be completed in one day and would require
4 
customers to be out of service multiple days or longer; and
5 
•
work could not be performed by KEDNY’s crews, requiring
6 
additional resources to contract for master plumber services and
7 
would require coordination of KEDNY’s service line replacement
8 
crews with additional plumber crews.
9 
Taken together, the Code requirements prevent KEDNY from performing the 
10 
meter relocation work.   
11 
12 
During 2018 and to date, KEDNY has suspended its meter relocation efforts to 
13 
pursue ongoing discussions with the NYC Mayor’s Office staff and the DOB 
14 
regarding whether there is a path forward that would enable KEDNY to perform 
15 
this work.  KEDNY has also updated Staff on this matter throughout the past 
16 
year.   
17 
18 
Q. 
What is KEDNY’s recommendation regarding meter relocations?
19 
A. 
KEDNY will continue ongoing discussions with DOB; however, at this time
20 
there is no clear path forward such that KEDNY can commit to meter 
21 
3838


--- Page 149 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of Gas Safety Panel 
Page 63 of 64 
relocations.  At best, KEDNY can look for opportunities to relocate meters in 
1 
coordination with customer-planned work on inside house piping where the 
2 
customer would be responsible for the replacement of the house-piping on the 
3 
inside of the building where the meter is removed.   
4 
5 
The Companies also note that KEDLI’s ability to continue relocations outside 
6 
of New York City may be similarly impacted by municipal building codes as the 
7 
program moves forward.    
8 
9 
D. SEP’s Concerns Regarding Radon
10 
Q.
Please address SEP’s recommendation that the Companies should provide 
11 
radon testing for its customers. 
12 
A.
The Companies agree that customer safety is paramount; however, SEP has 
13 
presented no credible evidence to suggest that the gas transported by the 
14 
Companies causes harmful radon emissions when burned inside homes for 
15 
heating, cooking, and appliances.  Respectfully, SEP’s witnesses are not 
16 
scientists or chemical engineers, and SEP has not presented testimony by any 
17 
witnesses who are experts in the fields of chemical engineering, radon, or the 
18 
composition of natural gas.  SEP cites only to a publicly-available article that 
19 
discusses increasing radon levels in Pennsylvania, where fracking occurs, to 
20 
support its belief that there could be unacceptable levels of radon emitting from 
21 
3839


--- Page 150 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of Gas Safety Panel 
Page 64 of 64 
natural gas far from fracking sites.  Absent compelling evidence that radon 
1 
emissions should be addressed by New York utilities, the Companies cannot 
2 
support spending customer dollars to address an unproven risk.   
3 
4 
Moreover, the Companies adhere to all currently applicable regulatory 
5 
requirements and standards for testing of the quality of natural gas entering their 
6 
systems.  To the extent that a scientific consensus is ultimately formed that 
7 
raises a legitimate concern over radon in the northeastern natural gas supply, 
8 
such an issue should be addressed statewide and for all local gas utilities.  SEP 
9 
notes that that legislation was proposed in 2013 in the New York State 
10 
Legislature related to radon concerns, but that legislation appears to have died 
11 
in committee and never passed.  The Companies’ rate proceedings are not an 
12 
appropriate forum to address this issue.    
13 
14 
Q.
Does that conclude your testimony? 
15 
A. 
Yes.

37

38

39

counties on Long Island.  KEDNY and KEDLI are parties to an agreement with 
1

2

3

4

5

6

10

11 
providers.

Q.
Please describe the Companies’ gas supply planning process. 
14 
A.
Typically, in the spring of each year, the Gas Supply Department develops plans 
15 
to meet the Companies’ gas supply obligation for the annual period from 
16 
November 1 of that year through October 31 of the following year.  This planning 
17 
process begins with an updated ten-year demand forecast that provides the 
18 
foundation for customer requirements that ultimately determine incremental 
19 
pipeline, storage, or peaking needs.

8 
Q.
What is the basis for the Companies’ city gate requirements?  
9 
A.
The primary firm demand (i.e., core customer load forecast) forms the basis for 
10 
the Companies’ gas supply portfolio.  The primary firm demand is the demand 
11 
imposed on the Companies by their firm customers, regardless of whether they 
12 
purchase gas commodity from the Companies or energy service companies 
13 
(“ESCOs”).  Pipeline and storage capacity, along with peaking assets, are used to 
14 
satisfy the primary firm demand.  An annual load duration curve or similar 
15 
approach is utilized to structure capacity contracts to best meet the shape and 
16 
frequency of the anticipated loads and to assure the Companies’ ability to meet 
17 
those loads.  The Companies do not incorporate any reserve margin assumptions 
18 
when developing their design weather forecasts and capacity requirement 
19 
determinations.

Q.
What is the role of underground storage in satisfying customer 
16 
requirements? 
17 
A.
Approximately 31 percent of the Companies’ normal winter supply obligation and 
18 
28 percent of their design day demand requirement are met by deliveries of gas 
19 
withdrawn from storage.  Under the Companies’ storage contracts, storage 
20 
deliverability typically declines as inventory decreases (known as “withdrawal 
21 
ratchets”).  Once reached, these ratchets cannot be reversed until the following 
22 
year.  Therefore, the Companies establish a storage withdrawal plan prior to the 
23 
winter season to maintain inventories at levels that allow sufficient storage 
24 
deliverability to meet forecast winter peak conditions (storage rule curve).    
25 
26 
Market area storage provides the Companies with services that cannot be easily 
27 
duplicated with other assets.  The most important attribute of storage assets is 
28 
flexibility, which is vital in serving changing customer requirements.  The 
29 
Companies’ Transco storage service contracts provide end-of-day balancing that 
30 
3873


--- Page 184 ---
Testimony of Elizabeth D. Arangio 
Page 9 of 45 
minimize the risk of over/under run penalties.  Whereas typical supply purchases 
1 
must be dispatched at the same volume for each day of a weekend or holiday, 
2 
storage allows enhanced flexibility by allowing intraday adjustments to meet 
3 
changing conditions.  In addition, storage improves the load factor of flowing 
4 
pipeline assets and is critical in meeting design weather conditions.  This value 
5 
cannot be duplicated by replacing storage with Marcellus production.  The 
6 
Companies utilize a least-cost dispatch to fill storage.  Where Marcellus shale 
7 
supplies present an opportunity to displace long haul supplies for storage refill, 
8 
the Companies take advantage of the economically priced supply. 
9 
10 
The Companies also have approximately 20 billion cubic feet (“Bcf”) of Gulf 
11 
Coast storage capacity.  These storage fields are made available to ESCOs as part 
12 
of the optional Gulf Coast storage release that occurs every April.  Assuming no 
13 
ESCOs opt-in for the Gulf Coast storage release, approximately 4.5 Bcf of the 
14 
total capacity is reserved for force majeure purposes.  The remaining 15 Bcf of 
15 
capacity, usually the entire KEDNY Washington Storage Service (“WSS”) field, 
16 
is utilized for off-system sales (“OSS”).  If all ESCOs opt-in, the capacity utilized 
17 
for OSS would be reduced accordingly. 
18 
19 
Q.
What is the role of liquefied natural gas (“LNG”) in the Companies’ 
20 
portfolio? 
21 
A.
The Companies maintain two on-system LNG facilities in Greenpoint, Brooklyn 
22 
and Holtsville, Long Island.  The Greenpoint LNG facility allows KEDNY to 
23 
3874


--- Page 185 ---
Testimony of Elizabeth D. Arangio 
Page 10 of 45 
store approximately 1.6 Bcf of gas and has peak day vaporization capability of 
1 
approximately 291,000 dekatherms (Dth) per day.  The Holtsville LNG facility 
2 
allows KEDLI to store approximately 0.6 Bcf of gas and has vaporization 
3 
capability of 103,000 Dth/day.  Collectively, the Greenpoint and Holtsville LNG 
4 
facilities provide the Companies with approximately 10 percent of their peak day 
5 
gas supplies. 
6 
7 
LNG provides the Companies with on-system services that cannot be easily 
8 
duplicated with other assets.  Because these resources can be brought on line 
9 
quickly, the LNG plants can be used to meet hourly fluctuations in demand, 
10 
maintain deliveries to customers, and balance pressures across portions of the 
11 
distribution system during periods of high demand.  Most importantly, these 
12 
resources are vital in preserving delivery pressures in the event that an off-system 
13 
resource becomes unavailable. 
14

expected to be provided by the Northeast Supply Enhancement (“NESE”) project

by December 2020.  There are no other planned changes related to swing services,

planning horizon.

A. Indigenous and Renewable Natural Gas

Q.

15

16

17

18 
into the distribution system. 
19 
20 
The Companies’ use of local production has not changed over the past several 
21 
years; the Companies continue to purchase any supply the Landfill can provide. 
22 
There is no forecast of Landfill production, but the Gas Control department

B. Northeast Supply Enhancement Project
1 
Q.
Do the Companies plan to add any incremental pipeline capacity in the next 
2 
five years to meet forecast design day load requirements?   
3 
A.
Yes.  Due to continued customer interest in conversions from oil to natural gas for 
4 
space and water heating, as well as increased demand from existing customers 
5 
and new construction in their service territories, the Companies have continued to 
6 
experience growth in the demand for natural gas.  The Companies’ ten-year load 
7 
forecasts show that demand for gas will continue to grow.  KEDNY and KEDLI 
8 
expect the demand for gas to grow at an annual rate of more than 1.3 percent and 
9 
1.0 percent for the next ten years, respectively.  The graph below shows the 
10 
balance between the forecast demand and the Companies’ supply portfolio for the 
11 
ten-year period ending 2027/28.  The graph shows that without significant 
12 
additional pipeline capacity, as further described below, supply falls short of the 
13 
demand. 
14 
3879


--- Page 190 ---
Testimony of Elizabeth D. Arangio 
Page 15 of 45 
1 
In response to the forecast growth, KEDNY and KEDLI signed precedent 
2 
agreements with Transco to deliver an additional 400,000 Dth of natural gas per 
3 
day to the downstate New York area.  To provide the additional supply, Transco 
4 
will expand its existing pipeline system along Pennsylvania, New Jersey, and 
5 
New York to connect to the Companies’ system in the Rockaway Peninsula.  The 
6 
NESE project will provide access to abundant and economical gas supplies, 
7 
thereby relieving supply constraints in the Companies’ service territories. 
8 
Furthermore, NESE will include a fully looped pipeline segment to interconnect 
9 
with the existing delivery lateral in New York Bay, further enhancing reliability 
10 
of the system.  The Companies have contracted for 100 percent of the incremental 
11 
pipeline capacity that will become available via NESE.

3880


--- Page 191 ---
Testimony of Elizabeth D. Arangio 
Page 16 of 45 
Q.
What will be the impact to the Companies’ ability to meet forecast demand if 
1 
the NESE project is not completed in time? 
2 
A.
In the absence of additional pipeline capacity, the Companies cannot continue to 
3 
add new gas load without creating an unacceptable risk of significant supply 
4 
shortfalls and corresponding drops in system pressure to below minimum 
5 
thresholds.  Such conditions will jeopardize the reliability of service and public 
6 
safety for the Companies’ existing firm customers.    
7 
8 
As can be seen in the graph above, the Companies’ current supply planning 
9 
assumes that the NESE project will be completed in time to meet the forecast 
10 
growth in demand for natural gas.  Currently, NESE is awaiting permit approval 
11 
from New York Department of Environmental Conservation.  If the project does 
12 
not become available by the 2020/21 winter season, the Companies will not be 
13 
able to prudently satisfy new or additional service requests without jeopardizing 
14 
the Companies’ ability to provide safe, reliable service to its existing firm 
15 
customers.  In that case, National Grid will have no choice but to impose a 
16 
moratorium on new and additional gas service in affected areas to maintain 
17 
system reliability.   
18 
19 
The GIOP direct testimony addresses NESE’s impact to the Companies’ capital 
20 
projects.  Notably, as mentioned above, the Companies will not be able to take 
21 
their LNG facilities out of service to complete necessary maintenance repairs if 
22 
NESE is not completed in time.

IV. 
Portfolio Management
1 
Q.
Please describe the Companies’ gas supply planning goals.   
2 
A.
The Companies’ primary gas supply planning goals are to: 
3 
(i)
Dispatch the gas supply portfolio assets under a least-cost strategy to
4 
reliably meet projected primary firm demand;
5 
(ii)
Maintain a diverse portfolio of gas supply, storage, and transportation
6 
capacity contracts with varying terms and pricing provisions; and
7 
(iii)
Implement a formal hedging program to mitigate price volatility.
8 
9 
These goals are consistent with the Commission’s “Statement of Policy Regarding 
10 
Gas Purchasing Practices” issued in Case 97-G-0600 and updated by letter issued 
11 
March 31, 2011.  The Companies maintain a portfolio that meets requirements 
12 
under design conditions while maintaining sufficient flexibility for mild winters. 
13 
14 
The Companies monitor these goals with regular meetings (monthly supply plan, 
15 
quarterly review, and annual RFP review).  Pursuant to Recommendation IX-4 
16 
from the final audit report in the Commission’s previous gas management audit 
17 
(Case 13-G-0009), the Companies established a process for the quarterly review 
18 
of gas supply procurement plans compared to actual purchases for a sample of 
19 
days during the quarter.  The review identifies variances in volumes and the use 
20 
of storage and delivery pipelines caused by weather, market conditions, 
21 
operational constraints, or other factors.  Variances are reviewed for patterns and 
22 
opportunities to improve the procurement process.  The Companies’ Energy 
23 
3882


--- Page 193 ---
Testimony of Elizabeth D. Arangio 
Page 18 of 45 
Procurement group has been conducting quarterly reviews since 2015.  Reviews 
1 
are attended by representatives from Gas Supply Planning, Gas Trading, and 
2 
Origination and Price Volatility Management. 
3 
4 
Q.
Please describe the Companies’ gas purchasing process. 
5 
A.
The Companies contract for quantities of gas to ensure sufficient supply to
6 
reliably meet design conditions, as well as to account for daily and seasonal load
7 
variations.  A combination of term and spot contracts provides necessary
8 
flexibility with respect to volume, which allows the Companies to respond to
9 
fluctuations in demand.  Both term and spot contracts are firm to ensure
10 
reliability.  The varying provisions in these contracts allow for pricing diversity as
11 
well.
12 
13 
 
Term Contracts 
14 
Term contracts have durations of longer than one month and less than one year. 
15 
These contracts generally have a specified fixed daily base load quantity 
16 
necessary to satisfy requirements under a “warm winter” scenario and may 
17 
include some summer storage refill quantities.  Other term contracts provide call 
18 
options to buy bundled gas supplies delivered either to the Companies’ city gates 
19 
or other upstream supply points.  KEDNY and KEDLI also utilize no-notice 
20 
storage to manage variations in load that allow the Companies to automatically 
21 
withdraw or inject gas at the end of the day to balance system load. 
22 
23 
3883


--- Page 194 ---
Testimony of Elizabeth D. Arangio 
Page 19 of 45 
Term contracts are generally priced monthly.  In addition to first-of-the-month 
1 
(FOM) published indices, monthly prices for some term domestic supply 
2 
contracts also use the New York Mercantile Exchange (“NYMEX”) last day settle 
3 
price plus a competitively bid location basis differential.  Monthly pricing ensures 
4 
the effectiveness of the NYMEX futures contracts entered as part of the 
5 
Companies’ hedging strategy. 
6 
7 
 
Spot Contracts 
8 
Spot contracts are firm contracts with a term of one month or less.  There are no 
9 
specific contractual triggers that require the purchase of spot gas.  These contracts 
10 
are made throughout the year to supplement term contract supplies, manage 
11 
demand variations due to weather, and maintain storage inventory targets.  Spot 
12 
contracts also provide pricing diversity (i.e., daily index vs. monthly index).   
13 
14 
The Companies purchase spot gas from a number of qualified and reliable 
15 
suppliers who have North American Energy Standards Board (“NAESB”) 
16 
contracts to minimize risk and obtain competitive pricing.  The amount of 
17 
capacity available depends on the time of year and storage availability.   
18 
19 
Daily spot purchases are priced either at reliable, daily published index prices or 
20 
at a negotiated short-term (daily) fixed price.  
21 
22 
3884


--- Page 195 ---
Testimony of Elizabeth D. Arangio 
Page 20 of 45

Q.
How do the Companies evaluate expiring contracts for gas supply?  
10 
A.
As decision dates for contract extension/termination approach, the Companies 
11 
determine the need to maintain and or modify (to the extent possible) each 
12 
contract as part of the resource portfolio.  The Companies use several criteria to 
13 
assess the need for transportation and storage contracts including but not limited 
14 
to: receipt point liquidity, reliability, complement to the existing portfolio, and 
15 
economics. 
16 
17 
Also, the Companies consider options to replace long-haul capacity with shorter-
18 
haul capacities.  For example, as supplies from the Marcellus shale region became 
19 
abundant and readily accessible, the Companies did not renew expiring long-haul 
20 
contracts with Union, TransCanada, and Empire pipelines that delivered more 
21 
expensive supplies from Dawn, Canada.   
22 
23 
3885


--- Page 196 ---
Testimony of Elizabeth D. Arangio 
Page 21 of 45 
While the current level of Transco long haul capacity is required to serve the 
1 
Companies’ peak day and peak season needs, it is not always the least cost option 
2 
for supply.  Recently, the Companies were able to reduce the path on Transco 
3 
long-haul contracts to reduce fixed costs.  Effective March 1, 2019, the 
4 
Companies have agreed to turnback 40 percent (27,473 Dth/day) of their 
5 
combined entitlements from Zone 1 (Sta 30).  Transco will allow the remaining 
6 
portion (41,210 Dth/day) to be turned back as early as October 2020.  After the 
7 
turnbacks, the Companies will still be able to reliably fill 100 percent of the 
8 
original contract volumes at downstream points.  The option to reduce capacity 
9 
paths is not one typically offered by the pipelines, so, when the opportunities 
10 
occur, the Companies will seek to take full advantage of such de-contracting 
11 
providing such options do not have an adverse effect on the reliability and 
12 
economics of the portfolio.

Q.
Do the Companies engage in off-system sales, capacity release and other
1 
arrangements to reduce their total gas costs?
2 
A.
Yes.  The Companies constantly monitor their gas resource portfolio to ensure
3 
that the appropriate mix of pipeline transportation capacity, market area storage,
4 
bundled city gate supply, and peaking resources are available to serve projected
5 
firm design requirements.  To further minimize costs, the Companies seek to
6 
optimize portfolio assets when they are not being utilized for the benefit of firm
7 
customers.  Except for Gulf Coast storage, for the Rate Year and beyond, the
8 
Companies have no plans to enter any pre-arranged off-system sales, capacity
9 
release, or streaming arrangements that would encumber upstream assets.  As in
10 
years past, once the winter heating season begins, the Companies will actively
11 
pursue opportunities to sell available supply and/or capacity in a manner that does
12 
not diminish overall supply adequacy, reliability or operational flexibility to firm
13 
customers.  For example, on warm days in shoulder months when pipeline
14 
capacity is not fully utilized, the Companies will seek to sell excess capacity to
15 
off-system customers.  Under this type of transaction, the Companies will
16 
purchase supply and transport gas on available pipeline capacity and then assess
17 
the associated variable costs plus a negotiated margin to the off-system customers.
18 
The Companies will also look for opportunities to execute physical pipeline trades
19 
where available.  Under such trades, the Companies would purchase firm supply
20 
delivered to the city gate on one interstate pipeline and sell a like amount of
21 
supply to another third party at a higher price on a different interstate pipeline.
22 
23

22

V.
Marginal Cost Studies
7 
Q.
Please describe Exhibit __ (EDA-8) – Estimated Marginal Commodity Cost
8 
of Gas For Period: November 1, 2020 through March 31, 2021.
9 
A.
Exhibit __ (EDA-8) shows the projected marginal gas commodity costs for the
10 
period November 1, 2020 to March 31, 2021.  By running two dispatch
11 
simulations, the marginal gas supply sources that would be dispatched to serve an
12 
incremental increase in customer demand were identified.  First, a baseline
13 
dispatch on the simulation model was prepared to establish the least-cost mix of
14 
gas supplies that would be dispatched to serve firm sales customer demand under
15 
normal weather.  Then, the simulation model was rerun with an increased
16 
customer demand of 1,000 Dth per day over the winter months (November
17 
through March) to identify those marginal supplies that would be dispatched to
18 
serve the increased demand.  The exhibit reflects the average monthly commodity
19 
cost of the marginal supplies that were dispatched.
20 
21 
3895

VI. 
Customer Choice Program
10 
Q.
Describe the Companies’ Customer Choice Program.  
11 
A.
The Companies’ Customer Choice Program provides customers the option to 
12 
purchase their supplies from Marketers.  There are two service options, Daily and 
13 
Monthly Balancing.  The Companies currently assign to Marketers at maximum 
14 
rates such interstate pipeline transportation and storage capacity as is necessary to 
15 
meet migrating firm customers’ load.  This practice is consistent with the 
16 
Commission’s August 30, 2007 Order in Case 07-G-0299.  City gate pipeline and 
17 
storage capacity contracted for core customers is assigned to Retail Marketers for 
18 
migrating customers in proportion to the anticipated design day load of the 
19 
customers.  All migrating firm customers are required to participate in the 
20 
Companies’ mandatory assignment program. 
21 
22 
3896


--- Page 207 ---
Testimony of Elizabeth D. Arangio 
Page 32 of 45 
Q.
What service classes are available to transportation customers?   
1 
A.
For KEDNY, transportation customers can receive service under Service 
2 
Classification (“SC”) 17 Firm and SC-18 Non-firm.  For KEDLI, transportation 
3 
service is available under SC-5 Firm, SC-7 Non-Firm, and SC-13 Non-Firm.   
4 
5 
Q.
How are transportation customer imbalances tracked and reconciled?   
6 
A.
When an ESCO customer’s meter is read, the customer’s account transaction is 
7 
sent from the customer system to the Companies’ Gas Transportation Information 
8 
System (“GTIS”).  GTIS then compares the ESCO’s expected deliveries, adjusted 
9 
for actual weather for the billing period, to the customer’s actual usage for that 
10 
same period.  The usage is prorated into the applicable calendar month based on 
11 
actual degree days.  Thereafter, a cash-out index price is applied to the difference 
12 
between the ESCO’s deliveries and customer usage.  Cancel and rebill activity 
13 
will be captured in the month in which the cancel or the rebill occurred as far back 
14 
as three years.  There have been no imbalance penalties assessed to a customer in 
15 
the last three years. 
16 
17 
Q.

18 
Commission's August 30, 2007 and March 28, 2008 Mandatory Capacity 
19 
Release Orders (in Case 07-G-0299) and FERC Order 712?   
20 
A.
The Companies have not experienced any issues from implementation of the 
21 
Commission’s Mandatory Release orders.  The Companies no longer have any 
22 
ESCO capacity that is grandfathered to the city gate. 
23 
3897

Q.

1

2

3

4

5

6

12

15

16 
•

17 
•
Iroquois – up to 50 percent
18 
•
Tennessee – up to 6 percent
19 
These modifications do not apply to power generation customers who have 
20 
negotiated points of receipt in their gas transportation agreements. 
21 
22 
3899


--- Page 210 ---
Testimony of Elizabeth D. Arangio 
Page 35 of 45 
Second, the Companies modified their operating procedures to allow ESCOs 
1

2

3 
capacity, ESCOs must notify the Companies by phone or email between 8:30 
4 
A.M. and 10:00 A.M. prior to the start of the effective Gas Day.  Between 10:00
5

6

7 
have received.  If there is any remaining capacity after allocation, it will be made 
8 
available on a first-come, first-served basis after 11:00 A.M. 
9

VII. 
Gas Cost Volatility Management
8 
Q.
What steps do the Companies take to mitigate the impact of gas cost 
9 
volatility on their customers? 
10 
A.
The Companies mitigate volatility in the gas commodity markets in several ways. 
11 
First, they maintain a balanced portfolio that includes contract storage.  This 
12 
allows the Companies to inject gas during the summer for withdrawal during the 
13 
winter, which enables the Companies to mitigate price volatility during the winter 
14 
when demand is greatest.  Second, the Companies maintain a geographically 
15 
diverse gas supply portfolio that helps to reduce exposure to volatility in any 
16 
single supply region.  Third, the portfolio incorporates pricing diversity that 
17 
minimizes exposure to volatility at a single pricing point or market index. 
18 
Finally, the Companies mitigate price volatility with a formal hedging program. 
19 
20 
Q.
Please describe the Companies’ hedging program.  
21 
A.
The Companies’ overall pricing diversity and volatility mitigation plan is to 
22 
protect prices for approximately 50 percent, but no more than 60 percent, of 
23 
3901


--- Page 212 ---
Testimony of Elizabeth D. Arangio 
Page 37 of 45 
forecast winter firm sales and to allow prices to float with the market for the 
1 
remaining forecast winter sales.  For this purpose, the “winter period” is defined 
2 
as November through March.  Prices are fixed through a combination of planned 
3 
storage withdrawals, which provide a natural hedge at the average price of 
4 
summer period injections, and financial hedging using NYMEX gas futures 
5 
contracts or NYMEX Over the Counter (“OTC”) financial settled swaps and 
6 
options using bilateral master agreements.  Beginning in the 2015-2016 winter, 
7 
the Companies are also using basis hedges for forecast purchases in the Northeast 
8 
producing region.  Locational basis swaps in conjunction with NYMEX hedges 
9 
are being used because of an observed disconnect between prices in the Marcellus 
10 
and Utica supply basins and NYMEX prices.  The use of these locational basis 
11 
swaps ensures that the Companies are mitigating volatility in the markets where 
12 
they purchase supplies. 
13 
14 
To determine the quantity to be hedged, the Companies forecast firm sales for 
15 
each month November through March, assuming normal winter weather 
16 
conditions, and multiply the results by 50 percent (Step 1).  Next, monthly storage 
17 
withdrawals to meet system operational needs are forecast and subtracted from 
18 
the result obtained in Step 1 (Step 2).  The results from Step 2 equal the quantity 
19 
of gas to be financially hedged to achieve the 50 percent target in each month.   
20 
21 
Once the volume of gas to be financially hedged each month is known, a monthly 
22 
hedging plan is created.  The purchases are spread evenly over 16 months starting 
23 
3902


--- Page 213 ---
Testimony of Elizabeth D. Arangio 
Page 38 of 45 
18 months prior to the start of each November.  Each financial transaction is 
1 
settled against the expiring month’s last day settlement price and the applicable 
2 
Inside FERC “First of the Month” index price.  The gross settlement payout or 
3 
gain is included in the gas cost for that month.  
4 
5 
The table below summarizes the percentage of forecasted gas supply hedged 
6 
physically and financially for November 2017-March 2018: 
7 
8 
Pricing Mechanism 
Downstate NY 
Physical (storage) 
30% 
Financial (swaps and options)
19% 
Index
51%
9 
The table below itemizes the number of financial swap (futures) and option 
10 
contracts hedged for the November 2017 through March 2018 winter season.  The 
11 
units for the data below are in contracts; one contract equals 10,000 Dth. 
12 
Pricing Mechanism 
Quantity 
Swaps
1,815

750
13 
Q.
Have the Companies’ hedging practices changed in the past year? 
14 
A.
Yes.  The derivatives market at Dominion South Point has developed with enough 
15 
liquidity so that the Companies can adequately acquire financially settled 
16 
Dominion South Point options.  The Companies have incorporated these options 
17 
in the winter 2018-19 hedge portfolio.  The Companies continue to monitor the 
18 
3903


--- Page 214 ---
Testimony of Elizabeth D. Arangio 
Page 39 of 45 
effectiveness of NYMEX and South Point for all receipt points in the forecast to 
1 
determine the most appropriate hedging location. 
2 
3 
Lower market volatility and market prices have translated to lower option 
4 
premiums.  Options provide superior downside protection versus swaps.  As a 
5 
result, “lessons learned” include favoring up to 100 percent of options for the 
6 
financial hedge component of the portfolios for the next winter 
7 
8 
Q.
Please explain how the Companies calculate gas price volatility. 
9 
A.
Gas price volatility is measured as the standard deviation of the lognormal of the 
10 
ratio of the monthly hedged price change through the winter, November through 
11 
March.  This standard deviation is compared against a similar calculation for the 
12 
NYMEX and South Point Natural Gas monthly settlement prices for the same 
13 
November through March period. 
14 
15 
Q.
Discuss how the Companies determine the success or failure of their gas price 
16 
mitigation program. 
17 
A.
The success or failure of the gas price mitigation program is determined by 
18 
comparing the physical and financial hedged prices with natural gas market price 
19 
indices over the same period.  The hedge program is expected to show lower 
20 
month-to-month price volatility than that of the market index. 
21 
22 
3904


--- Page 215 ---
Testimony of Elizabeth D. Arangio 
Page 40 of 45 
Q.
Please discuss internal reporting, oversight, and the audit structure of the 
1 
Companies’ gas price mitigation program.  
2 
A.
The Companies’ hedging program reporting and oversight is documented in the 
3 
US Energy Commodity Risk Management Policy.  National Grid’s US based 
4 
energy procurement activities are managed under the direction of National Grid 
5 
plc’s Finance Committee.  The Finance Committee authorized the Energy 
6 
Procurement Risk Management Committee (“EPRMC”) and delegated authority 
7 
to focus on energy risk, metrics, energy strategies, financial impacts and other 
8 
opportunities to the EPRMC.  The EPRMC provides Vice President level review 
9 
of strategies, with a focus on market risks, inclusive of price, credit, operational, 
10 
liquidity and reputational risks and Policy compliance.  The EPRMC serves to 
11 
administer a consistent and comprehensive sanctioning process for such strategies 
12 
and commitments across the organization.  The EPRMC has delegated some of its 
13 
authority to the Commodity Management Committee (“CMC”).  The EPRMC 
14 
appoints the CMC members and chairperson.  The CMC provides detailed review 
15 
of strategies, products and new opportunities with a focus on energy risk, metrics, 
16 
financial impacts and opportunities.  The CMC conducts research to understand 
17 
market rules, regulatory requirements, customer needs, risks, barriers to entry, 
18 
logistical requirements, competition, deal economics, risk measurement and 
19 
valuation requirements, all related requirements and exposures (including credit, 
20 
liquidity, tax and legal), and the impact of current and proposed strategies on 
21 
existing controls and limits, while administering a consistent and comprehensive 
22 
sanctioning process for such strategies and commitments across the organization. 
23 
3905


--- Page 216 ---
Testimony of Elizabeth D. Arangio 
Page 41 of 45 
The Companies review the hedging program and highlights any changes with the 
1 
Commission on an annual basis.  The Companies have a comprehensive internal 
2 
approval process to authorize a hedge strategy and maintain oversight 
3 
requirements.  Prior to execution, a hedging strategy is documented with details 
4 
that may include: risks to be hedged, volumetric targets, duration and cadence of 
5 
hedge plan transactions, and types of instruments to be used.  This documentation 
6 
is then reviewed and approved by the CMC prior to going to the EPRMC for 
7 
approval and authorization. 
8 
9 
The Companies employs the industry standard best practice of an independent 
10 
three-office model: 
11 
•
The Front Office develops and executes the hedge strategies hedges in
12 
accordance with the prevailing policies and strategies by entering into
13 
transactions with counterparties to mitigate natural gas price volatility.
14 
•
Risk Management is part of the Middle Office and maintains the overall
15 
control environment and assesses compliance with the Companies’ risk
16 
policy.  The Middle Office reports through the Treasury organization and
17 
provides a significant level of control and independent policing of the
18 
Front Office’s activities.  The Middle Office confirms all trades with
19 
counterparties and monitors the risk exposures of the deals, as well as
20 
verifying approved strategies are executed in accordance with the plan.
21 
•
The Accounting Department is part of the Back Office whose functions
22 
include processes in support of the Front Office, such as accounting,
23 
3906


--- Page 217 ---
Testimony of Elizabeth D. Arangio 
Page 42 of 45 
invoicing, check-outs, actualization, accounts receivable and payable, and 
1 
financial reporting.  The Back Office is also independent of both the Front 
2 
and Middle Offices and reports up through the Finance organization. 
3 
4 
Additionally, the Companies’ internal auditing department has performed audits 
5 
to ensure compliance, for example, with Sarbanes-Oxley. 
6

Testimony of Elizabeth D. Arangio 
Page 44 of 45 
Q.
What percentage of the Companies’ gas supply is physically hedged? 
1 
A.
As a result of planned storage withdrawals, which are based on normal weather, 
2 
approximately 30 percent of the forecasted November through March firm sales 
3 
demand is physically hedged.  The Companies do not hedge storage injections. 
4 
The Companies do not have any physical supply contracts with fixed price terms. 
5 
6 
Q.
How do the Companies use swaps/futures? 
7 
A.
The Companies use OTC swaps to execute fixed price hedged transactions.  OTC 
8 
swaps do not have any execution, transaction or commission fees.  The 
9 
Companies rely on credit thresholds in their bilateral master agreements to limit 
10 
the amount and frequency of margin calls associated with the daily mark-to-
11 
market valuation of each hedge transaction.  When the mark-to-market with each 
12 
OTC counterparty exceeds the credit threshold, the Companies use their various 
13 
credit facilities to meet the cash collateral margin calls. 
14 
15 
Q.
What types of options do the Companies use?  
16 
A.
The Companies use calls, puts, and collars. 
17 
18 
Q.
Describe how the Companies decide which types of options to use.  
19 
A.
When the underlying futures price is expected to fall, then call options are 
20 
preferred over swaps.  Collars may be purchased instead to reduce the premiums 
21 
paid or when the underlying futures prices are expected to be stable. 
22 
23 
3909


--- Page 220 ---
Testimony of Elizabeth D. Arangio 
Page 45 of 45 
Q.
Do the Companies place a limit on what they spend on options in any year?  
1 
A.
The Companies cap their option premiums at $13 million per year.   
2 
3 
VIII. 
Conclusion
4 
Q.
Does this conclude your testimony? 
5 
A.
Yes, it does.  
6 
7

III. 
NESE Project
6 
Q.
What is the current status of the NESE project? 
7 
A.
Since my supplemental direct testimony, both the New York State Department 
8 
of Environmental Conservation and the New Jersey Department of 
9 
Environmental Protection have issued a Notice of Complete Application and 
10 
concluded their respective public comment periods.  The Companies continue 
11 
to await the state agencies’ approval of the necessary permits to proceed with 
12 
the project.  Although the Companies continue to remain cautiously optimistic 
13 
that the NESE project will be approved, because the timing of approval is 
14 
uncertain, the Companies have adjusted their filings to reflect the 
15 
unavailability of the NESE project by the 2020-2021 winter season.  This does 
16 
not, however, preclude the possibility the NESE project could be approved 
17 
and become available.  If that occurs, the Company’s position is that the 
18 
Commission should consider the proposals and costs presented in the 
19 
Company’s direct and corrections and updates filings.

18

20

21 
3930


--- Page 241 ---
Case 19-G-0309

Page 4 of 11 
been a critical part.  Since as early as 2009, the Companies planned for the 
1 
construction of a metering and regulation station at Floyd Bennett Field 
2 
(“FBF”), which provided 647,000 Dth/day of takeaway capacity.  The FBF 
3 
station, completed in 2015, required an act of Congress to enact the New York 
4 
City Natural Gas Supply Enhancement Act of 2012.  The Companies signed 
5 
precedent agreements for two upstream projects to bring firm capacity through 
6 
the FBF station, first through the Northeast Connector project that provided 
7 
100,000 Dth/day of capacity, and then the New York Bay Expansion project, 
8 
providing another 115,000 Dth/day for a total of 215,000 Dth/day of capacity 
9 
through the FBF station.  The NESE project is the final piece that will bring 
10 
an additional 400,000 Dth/day of pipeline capacity to the Downstate New 
11 
York area.   
12 
13 
At the same time, the Companies have also invested in large on-system 
14 
projects to support the upstream capacity into the distribution system, 
15 
including the Brooklyn-Queens Interconnect project and more recently the 
16 
Metropolitan Reliability Infrastructure project.  These capital projects, both 
17 
upstream and on-system, have been a part of the Companies’ system and 
18 
supply plan and clearly communicated to all stakeholders through the years.  
19 
20 
3931

Page 5 of 11 
Other than an increased reliance on third-party peaking supply and local CNG 
1 
stations, the Companies have no viable alternative to the NESE project in 
2 
terms of traditional pipeline capacity – a fact that the CNY Policy Panel 
3 
ignores.  Furthermore, such stop-gap measures are short-term solutions and 
4 
will not address the ongoing supply constraint.  All other city gate stations are 
5 
at their maximum capacity, and increased reliance on third party peaking 
6 
supply creates a reliability risk of not being able to procure sufficient gas.  
7 
The only other option would be to pursue construction of a new station and 
8 
upstream pipeline expansion projects, which would not only impose 
9 
significant costs but also face the same regulatory hurdles that the NESE 
10 
project is currently experiencing.   
11 
12 
Although pipeline capacity cannot be entirely displaced in the near future, the 
13 
Companies have proposed several ways to reduce reliance on pipeline 
14 
capacity.  As presented by the Future of Heat Panel, the Companies proposed 
15 
various decarbonization initiatives including renewable natural gas projects 
16 
and enhanced energy efficiency programs.  Furthermore, consistent with the 
17 
Commission’s goals, the Companies developed new non-firm demand 
18 
response service classifications that will incentivize existing firm customers 
19 
and new customers to choose interruptible service that will relieve peak day 
20 
demand.  The Companies, however, emphasize that such initiatives and 
21

programs are part of a long-term plan to reduce carbon emission and do not

such measures cannot replace the volume of natural gas that the Companies

Supply Planning Process 
8 
Q.
Please summarize EDF’s recommendations regarding the Companies’ 
9 
current supply planning process. 
10 
A.
EDF recommends the following: 
11 
1. Companies should be subject to an annual gas supply process, with
12 
discovery rights for intervenors and technical conferences as needed
13 
(at 4).
14 
2. Companies should be required to submit a long-range plan, which
15 
would become the basis for evaluating future cost recovery (at 4).
16 
3. Companies should submit additional categories of information to assist
17 
the Commission in its review of gas supply issues (at 5).
18 
Q.
Do you agree with EDF’s recommendations? 
19 
A.
No.  As EDF notes (at 10-11), the Companies perform an annual gas supply 
20 
review, which includes filing of various data supporting the Companies’ long-
21 
3936


--- Page 247 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
Case 19-G-0310 
Rebuttal Testimony of Elizabeth D. Arangio 
Page 10 of 11 
term supply plan as well as an in-person meeting with Staff and interested 
1 
parties.  Each year, the review is performed under a new case number and is 
2 
available to the public.  Interested parties can become a party to the 
3 
proceeding as well as submit comments.  The Companies do not believe that a 
4 
formal evidentiary hearing is necessary to review the Companies’ annual 
5 
supply plan and will unnecessarily add time and cost with no significant 
6 
incremental benefit.    
7 
8 
Contrary to EDF’s assertion, the annual winter supply review is a public 
9 
process – the Companies file responses to Staff’s data requests in the public 
10 
docket, but with certain redactions for commercially sensitive and critical 
11 
energy infrastructure information.  Such redactions are necessary to ensure 
12 
public safety and the competitive position of the Companies and are provided 
13 
for exemption from public disclosure under the applicable Public Officers 
14 
Law.  The Companies do not believe that EDF’s suggestions for additional 
15 
information, technical conferences, and other procedural steps would provide 
16 
a clear incremental benefit to the existing process compared to the incremental 
17 
burden of cost and time.

11 
Green Gas Tariff Offering 
12 
Q.
What did the Companies propose with regard to a 
13 
“Green Gas” tariff? 
14 
A.
As discussed on page 31 of the pre-filed direct 
15 
testimony of the Future of Heat Panel, the 
16 
Companies propose a Green Gas Tariff offering to 
17 
commence in April 1, 2021, or the year following 
18 
the Rate Year, which the Companies refer to as 
19 
Data Year 1.  Under this offering, customers 
20 
could voluntarily purchase renewable natural gas 
21 
(RNG) for a portion or all their gas supply 
22 
needs.  The Companies state that different 
23 
customers prefer different pricing structures, 
24 
4014


--- Page 325 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SGSP 
7
where residential customers tend to prefer price 
1 
certainty while non-residential customers prefer 
2 
costs that scale as a percentage of use.  In 
3 
order to accommodate different customer 
4 
preferences, the Companies propose a tiered 
5 
system.  Specifically, page 32 of the Companies’ 
6 
pre-filed Future of Heat Panel testimony 
7 
provides four tiers: Low, Low-Middle, High-
8 
Middle, and High.  The proposed tariff would 
9 
provide customers with the option to choose a 
10 
tiered level of RNG at a flat rate per month for 
11 
residential customers, meaning those in service 
12 
classes (SC) 1A, 1B, and 1B-DG, and either a 
13 
flat rate or a percentage of monthly consumption 
14 
for non-residential customers, meaning those in 
15 
SCs 2, 3, 4, 7, and 21.  Participation in the 
16 
Green Gas Tariff would require residential 
17 
customers to commit to remain in the program for 
18 
a minimum of 12 months and non-residential 
19 
customers to commit for 24 months. 
20 
Q.
Explain how the Companies developed the tier 
21 
structure and the rate associated with each tier 
22 
A.
As stated in the response to IR DPS-365, the 
23 
Companies proposed tier structure was initially 
24 
4015


--- Page 326 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SGSP 
8
modeled based on the structure of a similar 
1 
program that was successfully implemented at 
2 
Vermont Gas Systems, Inc. 
3 
Q.
Have the Companies provided an update to their 
4 
tiered structure in their Corrections and 
5 
Updates filing? 
6 
A.
Yes, as shown on page six of the Companies’ 
7 
Future of Heat Panel corrections and updates 
8 
testimony, the Companies propose to “update the 
9 
residential tiers to include a flat-rate option 
10 
that more closely tracks the 100 percent option 
11 
for non-residential customers.”

Q.
Why do you recommend reducing the FTEs

associated with the green gas tariff program in 
24 
4017


--- Page 328 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SGSP 
10
the Rate Year? 
1

2 
available to customers until Data Year 1, it is 
3 
difficult to tell how many FTEs will be needed 
4 
to administer the program.  Therefore, we 
5

A.

15 
participation and RNG cost data, we are 
16 
concerned that a separate RNG reconciliation 
17 
could lead to bill impacts that may be 
18

19 
ultimately undermine their continued 
20 
participation in the program. 
21

When should the Commission consider a separate 
22 
reconciliation mechanism? 
23 
A.
When the Companies have some track record of 
24 
4018


--- Page 329 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SGSP 
11
forecasting the costs, so that the bill impacts 
1 
of the reconciliation can be understood.  Also, 
2 
the Commission should consider the number of 
3 
participating customers, across which the 
4 
reconciliation would be spread. 
5 
Q.
Why is it reasonable to have one mechanism to 
6

With the proposed program being in its infancy,

9

15

customers will be minimal and not have any

material effect due to the small expected RNG 
18 
volumes in the near future and the size of the 
19 
current gas supply portfolio.

21 
Gas Cost Hedging 
22 
Q.
How do the Companies mitigate gas cost 
23 
volatility associated with the gas supply 
24 
4019


--- Page 330 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SGSP 
12
portfolio for customers? 
1 
A.
As described starting on page 36 of the 
2 
testimony of witness Elizabeth D. Arangio, the 
3 
Companies use various methodologies to protect 
4 
prices for approximately 50 percent, but no more 
5 
than 60 percent, of forecast winter firm sales 
6 
and to allow prices to fluctuate with the market 
7 
for the remaining forecast winter sales.  These 
8 
methodologies are described in a diversity and 
9 
volatility mitigation plan. 
10 
Q.
Have the Companies recently changed their 
11 
hedging practices? 
12 
A.
Yes, as described starting on page 38 of witness 
13 
Arangio’s testimony, the Companies have 
14 
incorporated the use of options at Dominion 
15 
South Point into the hedge portfolio and are 
16 
favoring up to 100 percent of options for the 
17 
financial hedge component of the supply 
18 
portfolios for the upcoming winter.  The 
19 
Companies made these changes due to developed 
20 
liquidity at the market supply point, lower 
21 
option premiums and the downside protection 
22 
provided by options. 
23 
Q.
Do you support the use of 100 percent of options 
24 
4020


--- Page 331 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SGSP 
13
as the Companies financial hedge component? 
1 
A.
We support the Companies change because it 
2 
provides an added level of diversification, 
3 
which helps to mitigate gas cost volatility.  We 
4 
recommend that the Companies be required to 
5 
continue to monitor their hedging strategies, 
6 
including this one on an ongoing basis.  
7 
Decisions such as this one should be reviewed 
8 
with Staff during the annual winter supply 
9 
review. 
10 
Q.
What is the annual winter supply review? 
11 
A.
In the annual winter supply review, Staff 
12 
reviews the previous year’s forecasted gas 
13 
supply portfolio costs versus the actual costs.  
14 
Staff also reviews the gas cost forecast for the 
15 
upcoming winter heating season and, among other 
16 
things, Staff ensures that New York gas local 
17 
distribution companies comply with the 
18 
Commission Statement of Policy Regarding Gas 
19 
Purchasing Practices issued in Case 97-G-0600 on 
20 
April 28, 1998. 
21 
Q.
Please explain what is contained in that 
22 
Commission policy statement. 
23 
A.
The Commission Statement of Policy Regarding Gas 
24 
4021


--- Page 332 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SGSP 
14
Purchasing Practices establishes the requirement 
1 
for LDCs to consider diverse price volatility 
2 
mitigation strategies, among other things, as 
3 
part of its gas purchasing practices to defuse 
4 
price spikes and valleys for gas supplies. 
5 
Q.
Do the Companies current practices comply with 
6 
the requirements of the Commission’s policy 
7 
statement? 
8 
A.
Yes.  Our review demonstrates that the Companies 
9 
pricing diversity and volatility mitigation plan 
10 
is in compliance with the requirements of the 
11 
Commission’s policy statement. 
12

A.

12

13

16

17

19 
New York City Local Law 152, outside meters, 
20 
inactive accounts, methane leak pilot program, 
21 
enhanced contractor safety inspections, buried 
22 
vent lines, and annual reporting requirements.

PIPELINE SAFETY PERFORMANCE MEASURES 
3 
Q.
What is the purpose of pipeline safety 
4 
performance measures? 
5 
A.
The performance measures help to ensure reliable 
6 
service by requiring local distribution 
7 
companies (LDCs) to maintain focus on important 
8 
safety areas.  The performance measures for each 
9 
respective LDC are derived from that company’s 
10 
actual historic performance levels, our 
11 
knowledge of that company, and our experience 
12 
with other LDCs across the State. 
13 
Q.
Please explain the pipeline safety performance 
14 
measures currently in place for the Companies. 
15 
A.
Currently, the Companies have performance 
16 
measures for LPP removal, leak management, 
17 
damage prevention, emergency response, and 
18 
compliance with the pipeline safety regulations.  
19 
Pipeline Safety Staff has audited the Companies 
20 
performance and will recommend disposition of 
21 
any revenue adjustments. 
22 
Q.
Do these performance measures have associated 
23 
positive and negative revenue adjustments? 
24 
4037


--- Page 348 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SPSP 
10 
A.
Both LPP removal and leak management have 
1 
associated positive and negative revenue 
2 
adjustments.  The remaining performance measures 
3 
only have associated negative revenue 
4 
adjustments. 
5 
Q.
What is the total basis point exposure for the 
6 
Companies related to these adjustments? 
7 
A.
Each Company is subject to a total adjustment of 
8 
150 basis points for negative revenue 
9 
adjustments and a total of 15 basis points for 
10 
positive revenue adjustments. 
11 
Q.
Does the Panel recommend any modifications to 
12 
these performance measures and associated 
13 
positive and negative revenue adjustments? 
14 
A.
Yes.  We recommend positive revenue adjustments 
15 
for leak management, damage prevention, and 
16 
emergency response, removing the positive 
17 
revenue adjustment for LPP removal, and the 
18 
redistribution of the existing 150 negative 
19 
revenue adjustments between performance 
20 
measures. 
21 
Q.
Please describe the Pipeline Safety Performance 
22 
Measures Report. 
23 
A.
The Pipeline Safety Performance Measures Report 
24 
4038


--- Page 349 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SPSP 
11 
is an annual report presented by Department 
1 
Staff to the Commission.  The report summarizes 
2 
data, analyzes performance, and is used as a 
3 
tool to track and identify LDC performance in 
4 
the following areas of pipeline safety: damage 
5 
prevention, emergency response, leak management, 
6 
and compliance with the pipeline safety 
7

9

13 
That report contains data for calendar years 
14

16 
LEAK PRONE PIPE REMOVAL 
17 
Q.
What type of pipe is considered leak prone? 
18 
A.
LPP generally includes cast iron, wrought iron, 
19 
unprotected steel, and some early vintages of 
20 
plastic pipe that can become brittle.  Pursuant 
21 
to the Direct Testimonies of KEDNY and KEDLI’s 
22 
Gas Infrastructure and Operations (GIOP) Panels, 
23 
the Companies’ population of LPP consists of 
24 
4039


--- Page 350 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SPSP 
12 
unprotected steel, cast and wrought iron, 
1 
copper, and certain early vintages of plastic 
2 
pipe including Aldyl-A. 
3 
Q.
Please explain the importance of removing cast 
4 
iron pipe. 
5 
A.
In general, cast iron pipe is brittle, 
6 
susceptible to graphitization, which is a form 
7 
of corrosion, and has low beam strength.  Beam 
8 
strength refers to the amount of load a 
9 
structure can withstand before it fails.  The 
10 
low beam strength of cast iron pipe means that 
11 
the material can fail if it is subjected to 
12 
increased loading from a force pushing up or 
13 
down on it, or from a loss of ground support.  
14 
This characteristic makes the material 
15 
particularly susceptible to stresses from 
16 
underground forces or disturbances.  Such 
17 
disturbances include, but are not limited to, 
18 
ground settlement, freeze-thaw cycles, soil 
19 
erosion or nearby excavation activities.  The 
20 
physical characteristics of cast iron pipe make 
21 
it more prone to catastrophic failure than 
22 
cathodically protected steel or plastic pipe.  
23 
In addition, segments of cast iron pipe are 
24 
4040


--- Page 351 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SPSP 
13 
joined by hub joints, also known as bell and 
1 
spigot joints.  Packing material, such as jute, 
2 
was used in the joints to prevent leakage when 
3 
cast iron pipelines were first installed.  
4 
Unlike steel welded joints, these cast iron 
5 
joints develop leaks over time. 
6 
Q.
How does the removal of cast iron pipe 
7 
contribute to the safety of the natural gas 
8 
system? 
9 
A.
Cast iron pipe tends to be located in densely 
10 
populated areas where there are many enclosed 
11 
structures, subsurface structures, and 
12 
continuously paved areas.  In the event of a 
13 
major leak or pipeline failure, these 
14 
environmental circumstances may lead to greater 
15 
volumes of below-ground gas migration, 
16 
particularly under paved areas and through 
17 
connected subsurface structures, which creates 
18 
an increased risk for fires or explosions.  
19 
Removing this pipe reduces the potential for 
20 
incidents resulting from major leaks and 
21 
failures, thereby improving public safety. 
22 
Q.
What is meant by the term unprotected? 
23 
A.
Unprotected means that the pipe lacks adequate 
24 
4041


--- Page 352 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SPSP 
14 
cathodic protection, thus rendering it 
1 
susceptible to corrosion.  Unprotected steel 
2 
pipe often has inadequate or no coating, which 
3 
makes efforts to cathodically protect such pipe 
4 
ineffective and uneconomic. 
5 
Q.
How do LPP removal programs improve the safety 
6 
of natural gas systems? 
7 
A.
Leaks on underground piping can create safety 
8 
risks to the public and potentially lead to 
9 
serious incidents, resulting in injuries to 
10 
persons or property damage.  Therefore, removing 
11 
LPP reduces these safety risks. 
12 
Q.
Please explain the importance of removing 
13 
unprotected steel pipe. 
14 
A.
Data collected by the United States Department 
15 
of Transportation, Pipeline and Hazardous 
16 
Materials Safety Administration (PHMSA) shows 
17 
that corrosion is a leading cause of pipeline 
18 
failures, and that unprotected steel is 
19 
typically the pipeline material that is most 
20 
susceptible to corrosion. 
21 
Q.
What other benefits are associated with the 
22 
removal of LPP? 
23 
A.
Removing LPP should reduce the number of active 
24 
4042


--- Page 353 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SPSP 
15 
leaks, lead to a decline in leakage rates on the 
1 
distribution system, and reduce the operating, 
2 
maintenance, and overtime costs associated with 
3 
surveying leaks and responding to emergency 
4 
reports. 
5 
Q.
Please describe the LPP removal component of the 
6 
pipeline safety performance measures. 
7 
A.
This component is designed to ensure that a 
8 
company continues to proactively remove LPP from 
9 
its system.  Typically, a company would only 
10 
remove LPP to ensure compliance with the 
11 
pipeline safety regulations, to respond to 
12 
significant customer complaints, or through 
13 
coordinated municipal or state projects where a 
14 
portion of the associated costs are shared. 
15 
Q.
Do the Companies currently have LPP removal 
16 
programs? 
17 
A. 
Yes. 
18 
Q.
How do the Companies prioritize the removal of 
19 
LPP? 
20 
A.
The pipe to be removed from service is 
21 
identified and ranked using a risk assessment 
22 
model-based approach. 
23 
Q.
What is a risk assessment model? 
24 
4043


--- Page 354 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SPSP 
16 
A.
A risk assessment model prioritizes all segments 
1 
of LPP according to attributes that pose the 
2 
highest associated risk.  The model uses several 
3 
weighted factors, such as material type, 
4 
diameter, pressure, date of installation, and 
5 
leak history, to determine a ranking.  This 
6 
allows the Companies to provide a higher level 
7 
of public safety by focusing its efforts on 
8 
pipeline segments with higher risk.  Although 
9 
there are commonalities between the State’s 
10 
LDCs, it is important to note that the model has 
11 
been tailored to the Companies unique mix of 
12 
assets and may not be appropriate for other 
13 
LDCs.  A uniform approach cannot accommodate the 
14 
characteristics that must be considered for each 
15 
system. 
16 
Q.
Do the Companies remove leak prone services in 
17 
conjunction with their LPP programs? 
18 
A.
Yes.  When removing LPP, it is common to group 
19 
mains and services together within a single 
20 
project.  These efforts provide the most cost-
21 
efficient approach towards removing LPP, in 
22 
addition to minimizing the time a customer is 
23 
without service. 
24 
4044

Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SPSP

Q.
Please explain the term leak management. 
16 
A.
Leak management refers to a utility’s ability to 
17 
monitor and repair existing and newly discovered 
18 
leaks on its natural gas system.

Q.
Why are the Panel’s total and repairable leak 
8 
management targets reasonable? 
9 
A.
KEDNY and KEDLI use both historic and active 
10 
leaks as weighted factors when prioritizing 
11 
segments within its risk assessment model.  
12 
Therefore, the removal of LPP alone should 
13 
reduce leak rates.  As more and more of the 
14 
Companies’ systems are comprised of newly-
15 
installed piping, reductions in leak inventory 
16 
should be easier to attain.  Also, periodic 
17 
monitoring is required for all leaks on natural 
18 
gas systems.  This monitoring ensures that these 
19 
leaks have not become an increased threat to 
20 
public safety.  Monitoring such leaks requires a 
21 
physical visit to the location with work 
22 
required to be performed by a qualified person 
23 
using properly calibrated equipment.  The 
24 
4057


--- Page 368 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SPSP 
30 
reduction in the amount of this work, in 
1 
addition to the reduction of losses of natural 
2 
gas due to leakage, would result in less costs 
3 
for customers. 
4 
Q.
What does the Panel recommend with regard to the 
5 
associated negative revenue adjustments? 
6 
A.
Each Company should be required to meet its 
7 
annual repairable or total leak management 
8 
targets on at least one day from December 26th 
9 
through December 31st of the calendar year.  If 
10

11 
repairable or total leak management targets, we 
12 
recommend that Company incur a negative revenue 
13 
adjustment of 10 and five basis points, 
14 
respectively. 
15 
Q.
Why does the Panel consider the increase in 
16 
combined exposure from the current 12 basis 
17 
point to 15 basis points reasonable? 
18 
A.
The Panel recommends increases to the negative 
19 
revenue adjustments associated with leak 
20 
management targets by three basis points to 
21 
reflect the targets’ importance and the 
22 
corresponding risk unrepaired leaks pose to the 
23 
public.  The Companies’ expeditious repair of 
24 
4058


--- Page 369 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SPSP 
31 
leaking pipelines enhances system integrity and 
1 
safety and reduces methane emissions from the 
2 
leakage.  This reallocation is also consistent 
3 
with recent performance measures for other LDCs. 
4 
Q.
Why should the Companies be given from December 
5 
26th through December 31st of the calendar year 
6 
to meet their leak management targets? 
7 
A.
On an annual basis, the Companies strive to 
8 
reduce their respective leak backlogs while new 
9 
leaks are discovered daily.  Therefore, a five-
10 
day threshold should be established during which 
11 
each Company can meet its targets.  This 
12 
eliminates the potential for a target being 
13 
missed due to an increase of discovered leaks on 
14 
the last day of the calendar year.  Should the 
15 
repairable or total leak backlogs on any day 
16 
from December 26th through December 31st fall 
17 
below the established leak management targets, 
18 
the Companies would be considered to have met 
19 
the applicable target.  In addition, only 
20 
successful elimination of a leak will be 
21 
considered a valid leak repair. 
22 
Q.
What does this Panel mean by successful 
23

24 
4059


--- Page 370 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SPSP 
32 
A.
Successful elimination of a leak means that the 
1 
applicable company has either performed multiple 
2 
inspections, two or more, to confirm that zero-
3 
percent gas-in-air readings have been 
4 
maintained, or a recheck has been completed as 
5 
set forth in the Commission’s pipeline safety 
6 
regulations. 
7 
Q.

8 
A.
No.  Pursuant to 16 NYCRR §255.819(a), upon 
9

10

11

12

13 
follow-up inspection requirement for Type 3 
14 
leaks. 
15 
Q.
Although not required by the Commission’s 
16 
regulations, should the Companies perform 
17

18 
A.
Yes, this Panel recommends that the Companies 
19 
perform follow-up inspections for all Type 3 
20 
leak repairs and include these inspections in 
21 
their procedures. 
22 
Q.
How does the requirement that the elimination of 
23

24 
4060


--- Page 371 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SPSP 
33 
leak management measure?

2

4 
levels for performance.  However, until the 
5 
follow-up inspection confirms successful 
6

7

Q.

9

A.

19
