DAMAGE PREVENTION 
4 
Q.
Please explain the term damage prevention. 
5 
A.
Damage prevention refers to a utility’s ability 
6 
to minimize damage to its natural gas system 
7 
facilities caused by excavation activities.  
8 
Damage to a pipeline can result in the 
9 
uncontrollable release of natural gas and could 
10 
lead to an incident. 
11 
Q.
Please describe the performance measures related 
12 
to the prevention of excavation damage. 
13 
A.
Under their existing rate plans established in 
14 
the 2016 Rate Order, KEDNY and KEDLI are subject 
15 
to performance targets that measure the 
16 
Companies progress in minimizing damages to its 
17 
underground pipeline facilities and encourage 
18 
the Companies to continuously strive to improve 
19 
its performance.  These performance targets 
20 
include metrics for damage caused specifically 
21 
by mismarks and by the Companies and their 
22 
contractors, as well as the total number of 
23 
damages per 1,000 one-call tickets.  The total 
24 
4062


--- Page 373 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SPSP 
35 
damage category includes damages caused by 
1 
mismarks, by the Companies and their 
2 
contractors, excavator error, and damages that 
3 
occur when an excavator failed to notify the 
4 
one-call notification system of its proposed 
5 
excavation work. 
6 
Q.
What is the one-call notification system? 
7 
A.
The one-call notification system is an 
8 
organization that establishes and carries out 
9 
procedures to protect underground facilities 
10 
from damage due to excavation and demolition.  
11 
Pursuant to 16 NYCRR §753-3.1 of the 
12 
Commission’s pipeline safety regulations, 
13 
excavators are required to provide notice of 
14 
their intent to excavate to the one-call 
15 
notification system.  There are two one-call 
16 
notification systems in New York State; Dig 
17 
Safely New York, and New York 811.  Both 
18 
Companies service territories are covered by New 
19 
York 811.  Both Dig Safely New York and New York 
20 
811 record the pertinent information from the 
21 
excavator and transmit that information to the 
22 
member utilities that may be affected by the 
23 
proposed excavation work.  The affected 
24 
4063


--- Page 374 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SPSP 
36 
utilities then mark the location of their 
1 
underground facilities to put the excavator on 
2 
notice and allow the excavator to take necessary 
3 
precautions to avoid damaging the surrounding 
4 
facilities.  Each incoming call to Dig Safely 
5 
New York and New York 811 generates multiple 
6 
outgoing notices to the affected utilities, 
7 
including, but not limited to, natural gas, 
8 
electric, telecommunications, water, and sewer 
9 
companies.  The notice received by the utility 
10 
from the one-call notification system is 
11 
referred to as a one-call ticket. 
12 
Q.
What is a mismark? 
13 
A.
A mismark occurs when a utility fails to 
14 
accurately mark the location of its underground 
15 
facility in response to the one-call ticket.  
16 
Consistent with the requirements of 16 NYCRR 
17 
Part 753, and for the purpose of this measure, a 
18 
mismark is considered as any instance where the 
19 
markings are off by more than two feet.  It also 
20 
includes any instances where the utility fails 
21 
to mark facilities in response to a properly 
22 
requested one-call ticket. 
23 
Q.
What are damages by Company and Company 
24 
4064


--- Page 375 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SPSP 
37 
contractors? 
1 
A.
These are damages to Company facilities that are 
2 
caused by Company personnel or by contractors 
3 
who are directly working for that Company. 
4 
Q.
How does prevention of excavation damage benefit 
5 
public safety? 
6 
A.
These damages often cause interruptions of 
7 
service to customers, building evacuations, and 
8 
road closures. Explosions, fires, injuries, and 
9 
fatalities, while less frequent, have also 
10 
occurred as a result of these types of damages.  
11 
Therefore, reducing these types of damages 
12 
improves public safety.

Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310

1

EMERGENCY RESPONSE 
21 
Q.
Please describe the emergency response 
22 
performance measures applicable to KEDNY and 
23 
KEDLI. 
24 
4074


--- Page 385 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SPSP 
47 
A.
Pipeline safety regulations require each utility 
1 
to provide a monthly report of the total number 
2 
of reports received, and responded to, in 15-
3 
minute intervals during normal business hours, 
4 
weekdays outside of normal business hours, 
5 
weekends, and holidays.  These measures evaluate 
6 
utility response to gas leak, odor, and 
7 
emergency reports generated by the public and 
8 
non-Company personnel.  Staff and LDCs jointly 
9 
established the following standards for the 
10 
emergency response performance measures: respond 
11 
to 75 percent of all gas leak, odor, and 
12 
emergency reports within 30 minutes; respond to 
13 
90 percent of all gas leak, odor, and emergency 
14 
reports within 45 minutes; and respond to 95 
15 
percent of all gas leak, odor, and emergency 
16 
reports within 60 minutes. 
17 
Q.
What is the significance of the emergency 
18 
response performance measure? 
19 
A.
Leaks on inside piping, improperly operated or 
20 
installed appliances, and gas migration into a 
21 
building from leaks on outside piping all 
22 
present risks to the public.  The Companies 
23 
recognize this and dispatch personnel on a 
24 
4075


--- Page 386 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SPSP 
48 
priority basis in response to reports of gas 
1 
leaks, odors, or emergencies.  All LDCs are 
2 
required to maintain a log of such reports and 
3 
track the elapsed time between the dispatch and 
4 
arrival of qualified personnel responding to the 
5 
scene.  As the LDC’s response times increase, so 
6 
does the potential for an incident or safety 
7 
related condition to the public.  Therefore, it 
8 
is imperative that LDCs minimize their response 
9 
times to gas leaks, odors, or emergency reports. 
10

and emergency reports within 30 minutes; and six 
4 
basis points for responding to 90.00 percent or 
5 
greater of gas leak, odor, and emergency reports 
6 
within 30 minutes. 
7 
Q.
Is this positive revenue adjustment consistent 
8 
with that of other LDCs? 
9 
A.
Yes.  This proposal was first introduced by 
10 
Staff in Case 17-G-0239 for Niagara Mohawk, then 
11 
again in Case 17-G-0460 for Central Hudson, Case 
12 
18-G-0068 for Orange and Rockland.  The
13 
Commission adopted joint proposals including 
14 
consistent positive revenue adjustments in each 
15 
of those cases.  Staff also recommended similar 
16 
positive revenue adjustments in Case 19-G-0066 
17 
for Con Edison.  That case is still pending. 
18 
Q.
Why does Staff only recommend a positive revenue 
19 
adjustment for the 30-minute target? 
20 
A.
By their very nature, the 30, 45, and 60-minute 
21 
emergency response targets are all related; as 
22 
such, an improvement to the 30-minute 
23 
performance would directly result in 
24 
4081


--- Page 392 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SPSP 
54 
improvements to both the 45 and 60-minute 
1 
emergency response targets. 
2 
Q.
Should either of the emergency response targets 
3 
or the associated revenue adjustments expire? 
4 
A.
No.  The emergency response targets and 
5 
associated revenue adjustments should remain in 
6 
effect until changed by the Commission.

COMPLIANCE WITH THE PIPELINE SAFETY REGULATIONS 
9 
Q.
Where are the Commission’s pipeline safety 
10 
regulations located? 
11 
A.
The Commission’s pipeline safety regulations are 
12 
located within 16 NYCRR Part 255.  There are 
13 
also relevant statutory provisions in the 
14 
General Business Law and the Public Service Law. 
15 
Q.
How are violations identified? 
16 
A.
Pipeline Safety Staff conducts periodic audits, 
17 
investigates incidents involving natural gas and 
18 
liquified natural gas facilities, and 
19 
investigates complaints related to natural gas 
20 
and hazardous liquid pipeline systems.  
21 
Typically, when Pipeline Safety Staff discovers 
22 
an instance of non-compliance with the 
23 
Commission’s pipeline safety regulations, a 
24 
4082


--- Page 393 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SPSP 
55 
compliance meeting is held with the affected 
1 
company to detail the code section(s) related to 
2 
the instance(s) of non-compliance. 
3 
Q.
What is the purpose of the compliance meeting? 
4 
A.
The compliance meeting is an opportunity for the 
5 
affected company to provide information 
6 
clarifying any deficiencies found by Staff.  
7 
Such information might include explanations or 
8 
records that were not available at the time of 
9 
the audit or investigation. 
10 
Q.
How long does the affected company have to 
11 
provide this information? 
12 
A.
The affected company is required to provide this 
13 
information within five business days of the 
14 
compliance meeting.  After this period, Pipeline 
15 
Safety Staff reviews the information available 
16 
and issues a formal letter detailing the 
17 
specifics of the violations with respect to the 
18 
underlying regulations. 
19 
Q.
Is the Panel generally concerned with any 
20 
instances of non-compliance with the pipeline 
21 
safety regulations? 
22 
A.
In general, yes, this Panel is concerned that 
23 
any number of violations could indicate a lack 
24 
4083


--- Page 394 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SPSP 
56 
of control, issues with quality assurance, or a 
1 
culture that is willing to accept non-compliance 
2 
with the pipeline safety regulations as the cost 
3 
of doing business. 
4 
Q.
Do the Companies currently have mechanisms to 
5 
incent compliance with the pipeline safety 
6 
regulations? 
7 
A.
Yes.  In its Order Adopting Terms of a Joint 
8 
Proposal issued on June 13, 2013 in Case 12-G-
9 
0544, the Commission instituted a compliance 
10 
mechanism for KEDNY.  That mechanism applied for 
11 
calendar years 2013 through 2016.  In the 2016 
12 
Rate Order, the mechanism was updated for KEDNY, 
13 
and a corresponding mechanism was instituted for 
14 
KEDLI.  That mechanism applied for calendar 
15 
years 2017 through 2019. 
16 
Q.
Please generally describe the mechanism. 
17 
A.
KEDNY and KEDLI incur a negative revenue 
18 
adjustment for each instance, referred to as an 
19 
occurrence or violation, of non-compliance with 
20 
the pipeline safety regulations.  The amount of 
21 
the negative revenue adjustment varies based on 
22 
whether the non-compliance regards a high risk 
23 
or other risk provision of the regulations. 
24 
4084


--- Page 395 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SPSP 
57 
Q.
What is the difference between a violation and 
1 
an occurrence? 
2 
A.
Pipeline Safety Staff uses the term occurrence 
3 
to describe the number of instances that a 
4 
specific violation takes place, but for purposes 
5 
of this compliance measure, there is no 
6 
difference between the terms violation and 
7 
occurrence.  Historically, negative revenue 
8 
adjustment letters noted the violation of a 
9 
specific requirement and identified the total 
10 
number of occurrences that requirement was 
11 
violated.  The term violation is commonly 
12 
referred to in discussions and is widely 
13 
understood within the pipeline industry.  These 
14 
words are and can be used interchangeably. 
15 
Q.
How does the Panel account for violations for 
16 
which the Companies cannot provide a record? 
17 
A.
Pipeline Safety Staff considers it a violation 
18 
when requested Company records are not provided 
19 
or are found to be inaccurate. 
20 
Q.
How are violations categorized? 
21 
A.
Violations are categorized as either high risk 
22 
or other risk and are based on the likelihood of 
23 
risk to public safety.  High risk refers to code 
24 
4085


--- Page 396 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SPSP 
58 
requirements that, if not followed, lead to a 
1 
greater likelihood of an adverse impact on 
2 
public safety relating to loss of life or 
3 
property.  A breakdown of code sections by risk 
4 
category is provided in Exhibit__(SPSP-2). 
5

18

19 
or task.  Notwithstanding Pipeline Safety 
20 
Staff’s presence, violations of the minimum 
21 
pipeline safety regulations or the Companies’ 
22

23 
expects that the Companies’ performance is at 
24 
4094


--- Page 405 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SPSP

its best while being observed.  Therefore, any

4 
Q.
Why does the Panel recommend that the Companies 
5 
file and execute an implementation plan for more 
6 
than 10 record audit violations of a single code 
7 
section? 
8 
A.
The compliance mechanism provides a financial 
9 
disincentive for non-compliance with the 
10 
pipeline safety regulations.  If the financial 
11 
disincentive is insufficient to induce 
12 
compliance, such that a company violates a 
13 
specific code section more than 10 times, then 
14 
additional measures are necessary to change the 
15 
performance.  It is critical for the Commission 
16 
to be able to address all violations of its 
17 
pipeline safety regulations given the potential 
18 
for serious harm.  The implementation plan would 
19 
provide a specific mechanism to further this 
20 
goal and may avoid the need for formal penalty 
21 
actions against the Companies for each record 
22 
audit violation.  The Commission, however, 
23 
always has the authority to pursue a penalty 
24 
4095


--- Page 406 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SPSP 
68 
action to promote regulatory compliance 
1 
notwithstanding any financial disincentive or 
2 
reporting requirement that also may be in place. 
3 
Q.
Should either the compliance mechanism or the 
4

5 
A.
No.  The compliance mechanism and associated 
6 
revenue adjustments should remain in effect 
7 
until changed by the Commission.

18

20

21

22

23

24 
4096


--- Page 407 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SPSP 
69 
training programs? 
1

Yes.  The Companies must maintain continual 
2 
efforts to train first responders. 
3 
Q.
How do continual efforts benefit first 
4 
responders and, ultimately, customers? 
5 
A.
First responders would be better informed on how 
6 
to respond prior to an incident.  Continuing 
7 
these efforts would reinforce the actions first 
8 
responders should take, and those to avoid, 
9 
during an incident and would educate first 
10 
responders on how to safely operate certain 
11 
field devices.  Familiarity with equipment 
12 
should reduce response time and minimize the 
13 
potential for escalated incidents by reducing 
14 
errors made in a haste to render a situation 
15 
safe.  Also, improved training would enhance the 
16 
safety of the first responders by providing more 
17 
situational awareness of their surroundings and 
18 
the effects of their actions. 
19 
Q.
What does the Panel recommend the Companies do 
20

21 
A.
We support the Companies continued efforts in 
22 
their first responder training programs, and 
23 
further recommend that they adopt the principles 
24 
4097


--- Page 408 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SPSP 
70 
of the Pipeline Emergency Responders Initiative 
1 
(PERI).  Also, the Companies should conduct 
2 
regular drills with local fire departments and 
3 
municipalities and provide, under this case, an 
4 
annual report detailing its progress including, 
5 
but not limited to, the date and times of the 
6

7 
reviewed, and any applicable recommendations. 
8 
Q.
What is PERI? 
9 
A.

11 
incidents.

costs.

SERVICE LINE PROCEEDING 
1 
Q.
What is the service line proceeding? 
2 
A.
In its April 2, 2015 Memorandum and Resolution 
3 
Adopting Gas Safety Regulation Amendments, 
4 
issued in Case 14-G-0357, the Commission adopted 
5 
a new definition of gas service line, set forth 
6 
in 16 NYCRR §255.3(a)(43).  Under this new 
7 
definition, service lines extend to the outlet 
8 
of each customer’s meter, or at the connection 
9 
to a customer’s piping, whichever is further 
10 
downstream.  This includes meters located inside 
11 
a building and those inside each apartment 
12 
within a larger apartment building. 
13 
Q.
What are the impacts of this definition change? 
14 
A.
The primary impact of this change relates to the 
15 
Companies obligations to inspect piping inside 
16 
all buildings up to the gas meter, and to add 
17 
training, qualification, and testing for 
18 
individuals who make repairs on inside gas 
19 
piping, upstream of the meter. 
20 
Q.
Did the Companies address the impacts from the 
21 
service line proceeding in their testimony? 
22 
A.
Yes.  On pages 17 through 20 of the Gas Safety 
23 
Panel’s direct testimony, KEDNY and KEDLI 
24 
4101


--- Page 412 ---

2

3

4 
resulting from the inspections, and additional 
5 
full-time equivalent inspectors. 
6

When should the Companies perform these 
7

8

9 
most of the inspections during their normal

12 
reconnections.  For some locations with inside 
13

NEW YORK CITY LOCAL LAW 152 
8 
Q.
What is New York City Local Law 152?  
9 
A.
In 2016, the New York City Council passed Local 
10 
Law 152 (Local Law 152), which requires owners 
11 
of buildings in New York City, other than those 
12 
classified in occupancy group R-3, to perform 
13 
periodic inspections of their buildings’ natural 
14 
gas piping.  Occupancy group R-3 building are 
15 
primarily residential buildings with no more 
16 
than two dwelling units.  The natural gas piping 
17 
inspected under Local Law 152 would include the 
18 
jurisdictional inside piping from the service 
19 
line definition change.  The inspections 
20 
required by Local Law 152 would include 
21 
examinations for atmospheric corrosion and 
22 
testing for leakage. 
23 
Q.
What is the status of Local Law 152? 
24 
4104


--- Page 415 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SPSP 
77 
A.
The New York City Department of Buildings (DOB), 
1 
which is the agency with primary enforcement 
2 
responsibility, issued proposed rules on 
3 
November 30, 2018.  Comments on the new rules 
4 
were due on January 2, 2019.  As it stands, the 
5 
proposed rules have several issues that would, 
6 
if adopted, require duplicative inspections, one 
7 
performed to meet requirements on the building 
8 
owner and a second one performed to meet 
9 
requirements on the utility pursuant to State 
10 
and Federal regulations, based on the definition 
11 
of service line.  This outcome would be 
12 
inefficient, burdensome, and more expensive to 
13 
customers. 
14 
Q.
Explain the issues with the current rule as 
15 
identified by the Panel. 
16 
A.
There are three issues with the DOB rules as 
17 
proposed which prevent the inside inspections 
18 
required by building owners from satisfying the 
19 
Companies’ obligations to perform inspections 
20 
under the new service line definition.  The 
21 
first issue is that the DOB regulations do not 
22 
require the plumber performing the inside 
23 
inspections to meet minimum training and 
24 
4105


--- Page 416 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SPSP 
78 
qualification standards that require persons 
1 
performing these inspections to be operator 
2 
qualified and to be part of a drug and alcohol 
3 
testing program; both of which are requirements 
4 
of the pipeline safety regulations.  Without 
5 
those requirements, the inspections performed by 
6 
the plumber would not be valid for the 
7 
inspections required of the Companies. 
8 
Q.
What does this Panel mean when it uses the term 
9 
plumber? 
10 
A.
We refer to licensed master plumbers or 
11 
individuals under the direct and continuing 
12 
supervision of licensed master plumbers, as 
13 
stated in Local Law 152 §28-318.3. 
14 
Q.
What is the second concern with the current 
15 
proposed rules? 
16 
A.
The proposed rules do not explicitly require 
17 
inside inspections for natural gas piping in 
18 
tenant spaces, for example, within apartments.  
19 
If the proposed rules do not require inspections 
20 
within tenant spaces, the inside piping 
21 
associated with meters inside apartments would 
22 
not be inspected by the plumber and would 
23 
require a separate inspection by the Companies. 
24 
4106


--- Page 417 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SPSP 
79 
Q.
What is the third issue with the current 
1 
proposed rules? 
2 
A.
The timeframes proposed by the DOB do not align 
3 
with the inspection timeframes prescribed by the 
4 
pipeline safety regulations.  Local Law 152 
5 
requires inspections once every five years but 
6 
allows for modifications to the timeframe by the 
7 
Commissioner of the DOB if the Public Service 
8 
Commission adopts an alternate inspection 
9 
schedule.  In contrast, the rules proposed by 
10 
the DOB require an inspection once every five 
11 
years with no option or opportunity for 
12 
modification.  KEDNY and KEDLI perform leakage 
13 
surveys in business districts on an annual basis 
14 
and in non-business districts every three years.  
15 
As a result, the DOB inspections will be 
16 
scheduled on a different cycle than those 
17 
inspections required to be performed by the 
18 
Companies. 
19 
Q.
Given these issues, what does this Panel 
20 
recommend? 
21 
A.
The Panel recommends that KEDNY and KEDLI 
22 
continue to work with DOB to further align the 
23 
proposed rules implementing Local Law 152 with 
24 
4107


--- Page 418 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SPSP 
80 
the inspection requirements of the service line 
1

adopted as issued without modification, we 
3 
recommend that KEDNY and KEDLI be required to 
4 
file a plan, within 60 days of issuance of the 
5 
DOB’s final rule or within 60 days of the 
6 
Commission’s order setting rates in these 
7 
proceedings, whichever is later, detailing how 
8 
the Companies will work with building owners to 
9 
encourage the use plumbers who can perform the

inspections in compliance with the requirements 
11 
of the pipeline safety regulations.  This could 
12 
include providing equipment that electronically

15 
to hire licensed master plumbers that are 
16 
operator qualified and under a drug and alcohol

OUTSIDE METERS 
8 
Q.
What are the benefits of outside meters? 
9 
A.
The primary benefit is that outside meters are 
10 
safer than inside meters.  In the event of an 
11 
emergency, outside meters allow utility 
12 
personnel and/or emergency responders to quickly 
13 
shut off the flow of gas without entering the 
14 
building and without having to locate the curb 
15 
valve.  An additional benefit is that a utility 
16 
does not require access to the premises in order 
17 
to perform the required leakage surveys and 
18 
atmospheric corrosions inspections. 
19 
Q.
Do the Companies have programs to move meters 
20 
outside? 
21 
A.
Yes, pursuant to the 2016 Rate Orders, KEDNY and 
22 
KEDLI are required to move meters outside 
23 
congruent with any planned service line 
24 
4109


--- Page 420 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SPSP 
82 
replacements, new service installations, or 
1 
under other circumstances that offer the 
2 
customer and the Companies the opportunity to 
3 
relocate the meter outside. 
4 
Q.
What does the Panel recommend? 
5 
A.
We recommend that the Companies be required to 
6 
move meters outside whenever they perform any 
7 
service line replacements, either by insertion 
8 
or direct burial.  This would be for any reason, 
9 
for example, as part of LPP removals or to 
10 
repair a leak.  In addition, KEDNY and KEDLI 
11 
should also move meters outside whenever it 
12 
performs service transfers, which is when it 
13 
moves an existing service line to a newly 
14 
installed pipeline.  The Panel recommends that 
15 
the Companies be required to submit an annual 
16 
report indicating the amount of service work it 
17 
performed in that year and the number of meters 
18 
it moved outside as part of that work. 
19 
Q.
Why does the Panel recommend this? 
20 
A.
When the Companies perform service work, they 
21 
have a construction crew on site and often are 
22 
already interrupting service to the customer.  
23 
Moving the meter outside at this time is the 
24 
4110


--- Page 421 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SPSP 
83 
most convenient, cost effective, and practical 
1 
time to do so.  Since the Companies replace a 
2 
leak-prone service with modern materials, it is 
3 
unlikely it will return to perform additional 
4 
work in the near future.  Therefore, the 
5 
Companies should avail themselves of the 
6 
opportunity to relocate the meter outside at the 
7 
time of replacement.

3

operations for physical disconnection, and the 
5 
legal replevin process used to secure access to

8

10

11

12

13

14

21

9

10 
A.
The Commissions’ regulation, 16 NYCRR 
11 
§255.727(d), requires one of following: closing
12 
the valve to prevent the flow of gas with a 
13 
locking device or other means designed to 
14 
prevent the opening of the valve by persons 
15 
other than those authorized by the operator; 
16 
install a mechanical device or fitting that will 
17 
prevent the flow of gas in the service line or 
18 
in the meter assembly; or physically disconnect 
19 
the building’s or unit’s piping from the gas 
20 
supply and seal the open pipe ends. 
21

22

23 
A.

24 
4113

86 
safety regulations, it is important to note that 
1

3

4 
soon as practicable.  The regulations do require 
5 
each segment of pipeline that becomes unsafe to 
6 
be replaced, repaired, or removed from service, 
7 
and specifically requires certain action take 
8 
place when service is discontinued.

18 
through pipelines.  Should a regulator 
19 
experience an over pressure situation, the vent 
20 
line would transport the excess natural gas to a

22 
atmosphere outside of the building.  Should a

Q.

4

5

6

14

18

20 
further downstream where a meter is located

within the building; if a meter is located

23 
deemed to terminate at the outside of the

2 
A.

3 
Companies’ efforts with regards to buried vent 
4 
lines as acceptable.  At a minimum, buried vent 
5 
lines should have already been identified by the

7 
line inspection programs, but as a part of their 
8 
operating and maintenance plans.  Thus, any 
9 
requested funding for the buried vent line 
10 
programs should be borne by the shareholders and 
11 
not the customers due to the Companies failures 
12 
to adequately address the vent lines in the 
13 
past.
